Section

7.1
presents preliminary notions for rPAs.
In
Section

7.2
, we examine the applicability of AG rules for general rPAs and present counterexamples to the asymmetric AG rule for (i) memoryless nature, and (ii) non-convex uncertainty sets.

Section

7.3
restricts to convex rPAs with memory-full nature and identifies a relation to (possibly uncountably branching) PAs.
We recover compositional AG rules by introducing a convexity-preserving composition operator
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
.
Finally,
Section

7.4
considers rPAs with interval transitions and a common interval-arithmetic relaxation of the parallel composition (e.g.,
[
46
,
30
]
) that preserves the interval structure while introducing spurious distributions
[
57
]
.
We show that, under this relaxed composition, the
AG framework of
[
44
]
—in particular the asymmetric rule—cannot be lifted directly.

7.1.
Robust probabilistic automata

We introduce
robust probabilistic automata (rPAs)
by combining rMDPs (e.g.,
[
3
, Definition 3.25]
) and Segala’s PAs
[
59
]
.
The transition function and the action structure follow our definition of pPAs, see
Sections

2.1
and
1
.

{defi}
A
robust probabilistic automaton
(rPA) over finite alphabet
\Sigma
is a tuple
\mathcal{U}=\left(S_{\mathcal{U}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{\mathcal{U}}},\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}},L_{\mathcal{U}}\right){}
, where
S_{\mathcal{U}}
,
\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{U}}
,
{s^{\mathit{init}}_{\mathcal{U}}}\in S_{\mathcal{U}}
, and
L_{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}})\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
are as for pPAs and the partial function

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}}\colon(S_{\mathcal{U}}\times\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{U}})\hookrightarrow 2^{\mathit{Dist}(S_{\mathcal{U}})},

is an
uncertain transition function
mapping each state–action pair
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}})
to a non-empty set
\mathbf{P}_{s,\alpha}\subseteq\mathit{Dist}(S_{\mathcal{U}})
of successor distributions.

We may drop the subscript
\mathcal{U}
, if it is clear from the context.
For a state
s\in S
and an action
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
, the set
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\subseteq\mathit{Dist}(S)
is called an
uncertainty set
.
As we assume a finite state space
S
, all probability distributions in
\mathit{Dist}(S)
have discrete, finite support.

Remark 19
.

The above definition assumes that the uncertainty sets

\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\subseteq\mathit{Dist}(S)
for different
state–action pairs
(s,\alpha)
are assumed to be independent—following the standard
(s,\alpha)
rectangularity assumption for robust or parametric MDPs in the literature
[
66
,
3
]
.

A set
D\subseteq\mathit{Dist}(S_{\mathcal{U}})
is
convex
if it is equal to its convex hull, i.e.,
D=\mathit{conv}\!\left(D\right)
, where

\mathit{conv}\!\left(D\right)~=~\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda(i)\cdot\mu_{i}~\Big|~k\in\mathbb{N}\,,~\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}\}\subseteq D\,,~\lambda\in\mathit{Dist}(\{1,\dots,k\})\Big\}.

The set of
extreme points

\mathit{ext}\!\left(D\right)
of a convex set
D
is the set of distributions that can not be expressed as a convex combination of the remaining elements of
D
, i.e.,

\mathit{ext}\!\left(D\right)~=~\Big\{\mu\in D~\Big|~\mathit{conv}\!\left(D\setminus\{\mu\}\right)\neq D\Big\}.

Convex set
D
is
polytopic
, if it coincides with the convex hull of its finitely many extreme points, i.e.,
|\mathit{ext}\!\left(D\right)|<\infty
and
D=\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathit{ext}\!\left(D\right)\right)
.

{defi}

An rPA is called
convex
(
polytopic
), if for every
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P})
the uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\subseteq\mathit{Dist}(S)
is convex (polytopic).

s_{0}

s_{1}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{2},1]
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[0,\frac{1}{2}]
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}

(a)

rPA
\mathcal{U}_{1}

t_{0}

t_{1}

t_{2}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}]
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}]
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\frownie

(b)

rPA
\mathcal{U}_{2}

Figure 5
.
Example robust PAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
.

{exa}

Consider the rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
depicted in
Figures

5(a)
and
5(b)
, respectively.
The alphabet of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
is
\Sigma_{1}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}
and for
\mathcal{U}_{2}
it is
\Sigma_{2}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c},\frownie\}
.

In all rPA figures, we display only transition labels and omit the underlying actions, since in the depicted rPAs the actions are uniquely determined by the transition label.
By misuse of notation, we often write
\mathbf{P}(s,\mathsf{a})
as shorthand for
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
, where
\alpha
is the unique action with
L(s,\alpha)=\mathsf{a}
.

The uncertainty sets in
Figure

5
are given in interval form.
Informally, an annotation of the form
[\ell,u]
next to a transition from state
s
to a successor state
s^{\prime}
indicates that for every distribution
\mu
in the corresponding uncertainty set we have
\ell\leq\mu(s^{\prime})\leq u
; the probabilities for all successors of that transition must sum to 1.
For instance, in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
the uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}(s_{0},\mathsf{a})
contains exactly those mappings
\mu\colon\{s_{0},s_{1}\}\to[0,1]
with
\mu(s_{0})+\mu(s_{1})=1
(i.e.,
\mu\in\mathit{Dist}(\{s_{0},s_{1}\})
) and for which
0\leq\mu(s_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq\mu(s_{1})\leq 1
.

All uncertainty sets in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
are polytopic uncertainty sets.
In particular, both
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
are
interval probabilistic automata
(iPAs), a special type of rPAs in which each uncertainty set is defined by lower and upper bounds on transition probabilities.
We further elaborate on iPAs in
Section

7.4
.

Infinite and finite paths of an rPA are defined analogously to pPAs:
An
infinite path
of
\mathcal{U}
is an alternating sequence
\pi=s_{0},\alpha_{0},s_{1},\alpha_{1},\ldots
of states
s_{i}\in S
and actions
\alpha_{i}\in\mathit{Act}
such that
s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}
and
(s_{i},\alpha_{i})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P})
for all
i\geq 0
.
A
finite path
of length
n\in\mathbb{N}
is a prefix
\widehat{\pi}=s_{0},\alpha_{0},\ldots,s_{n}
of an infinite path, ending in a state
\mathit{last}(\widehat{\pi})=s_{n}
.
For a (finite or infinite) path
\widehat{\pi}
we write
|\widehat{\pi}|\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
for its length and
\pi[0,j]
for its prefix of length
j\leq|\widehat{\pi}|
.
We write
\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}
and
\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}
for the sets of infinite and finite paths of
\mathcal{U}
, respectively.

As for PAs, strategies resolve nondeterminism over actions by assigning (sub)distributions to the enabled actions as a function of the history.
We allow partial strategies that, intuitively, may assign probability
0
to all enabled actions, modelling that no further transition is taken.

The key difference to PAs is that in rPAs the probabilistic choice between successor states is not fixed by the transition function: once a strategy has selected an action, a separate (non-probabilistic) nature player chooses a concrete successor distribution from the corresponding uncertainty set.

{defi}
A
nature
for
\mathcal{U}
is a function
\nu\colon\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}\times\mathit{Act}\to\mathit{Dist}(S)
such that
\nu(\pi,\alpha)\in\mathbf{P}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)
.
The set of all natures on
\mathcal{U}
is denoted by
\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}
.

A
memoryless nature
only depends on the last state of the path, i.e.,
\nu(\pi,\alpha)=\nu(\pi^{\prime},\alpha)
whenever
\mathit{last}(\pi)=\mathit{last}(\pi^{\prime})
.
The set of memoryless natures on
\mathcal{U}
is denoted by
\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\mathit{mless}}
.

We simply write
\mathit{Nat}_{\!}^{\!}
or
\mathit{Nat}_{\!}^{\!\mathit{mless}}
when the rPA
\mathcal{U}
is clear from the context.

Remark 20
.

In our setting, nature is
non-probabilistic
: for each
(\pi,\alpha)
it deterministically
selects a single distribution from
\mathbf{P}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)
.
Probabilistic nature formalisations do not gain expressiveness:
Allowing nature to choose a distribution from an uncertainty set according to another probability distribution is equivalent to replacing all uncertainty sets with their convex hull.

Remark 21
.

In the literature, a memoryless nature that fixes one distribution per state–action pair is referred to as
once-and-for-all
semantics
[
64
,
2
]
.
Further variants are possible, such as (dynamic) Markovian natures
[
3
,
64
]
that additionally depend on the length of the path, or more general semantics that allow branching and merging of states
[
2
]
.
We focus on the stepwise setting with memoryless or memory-full (history-dependent) nature and leave other variants to future work.

We lift various established notions from (p)PAs to rPAs.
Strategies for rPAs are defined analogously to strategies for (p)PAs (
Section

2.1
).
We write
Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\mathit{prt}}
and
Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\mathit{cmp}}
for the sets of partial and complete strategies on
\mathcal{U}
, respectively, and use
Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}
when
\star\in\{\mathit{prt},\mathit{cmp}\}
.
The
(sub-)probability measure

Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}
for a fixed nature
\nu
and a strategy
\sigma
for
\mathcal{U}
on the measurable subsets of
\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}
is obtained by a standard cylinder-set construction
[
4
]
.
For a finite path
\widehat{\pi}=s_{0},\alpha_{0},\ldots,s_{n}
of
\mathcal{U}
, we set

Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\textsf{Cyl}(\widehat{\pi})\right)~=~\llbracket\,s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}\,\rrbracket\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\sigma(\widehat{\pi}[0,i],\alpha_{i})\cdot\nu{}(\widehat{\pi}[0,i],\alpha_{i})(s_{i+1}).

This definition extends uniquely to a (sub-)probability measure on all measurable subsets of
\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}
.

Remark 22
.

The possible uncountability of the uncertainty set does not cause measure-theoretic difficulties: by definition, all successor distributions
\nu(\widehat{\pi},\alpha)
and all strategy choices
\sigma(\widehat{\pi})
are discrete distributions and thus have at most countable support.

We lift
Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}
to (sets of) finite paths and write, e.g.,
Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\widehat{\pi}\right)
for
\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}
or
Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\Pi\right)
for
\Pi\subseteq\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}
, implicitly referring to (unions of) cylinder sets.

Probabilistic objectives for rPAs and their satisfaction are defined analogously to
Section

4
.
Satisfaction of an objective
\mathbb{P}_{\sim p}\!(\mathcal{L})
with
{\sim}\in\{>,\geq,<,\leq\}
,
p\in[0,1]
, and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{\infty}
, by rPA
\mathcal{U}
nature
\nu
and strategy
\sigma
is defined as

\mathcal{U},\nu,\sigma\models{}\!\mathbb{P}_{\sim p}\!(\mathcal{L})\ \Leftrightarrow\ Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\sim p.

We distinguish two nature semantics:
memory-full
and
memoryless
nature.

{defi}

Let
\star\in\{\mathit{prt},\mathit{cmp}\}
and let
\varphi
be an objective.
The
satisfaction relation

\models^{\star}
for rPA
\mathcal{U}
is given by:

\mathcal{U}\models^{\star}\varphi{}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\forall\nu\in\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}:\forall\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}:\mathcal{U},\nu,\sigma\models{}\!\varphi{}.

Satisfaction under memoryless nature—denoted
\models^{\star}_{\mathit{mless}}
—is defined analogously w.r.t.
\mathit{Nat}_{\!}^{\!\mathit{mless}}
.
Satisfaction of multi-objective properties is defined as for pPAs (
Section

4
).

We denote AG triples for rPAs as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}\models^{\star}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\left(\forall\nu\in\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}:\forall\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}:\quad\mathcal{U},\nu,{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}\quad\rightarrow\quad\mathcal{U},\nu,{\sigma}\models\textsf{G}\right),

for
\star\in\{\mathit{cmp},\mathit{prt}\}
.
AG triples for memoryless nature are denoted

{\mathcal{U}}\models^{\star}_{\mathit{mless}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}

and are defined analogously, but with respect to the set of memoryless natures
\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\mathit{mless}}
.

{defi}

[Alphabet Extension for rPAs]Let
\mathcal{U}=\left(S,{s^{\mathit{init}}},\mathit{Act},\mathbf{P},L\right)
be a rPA over
\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
and let
\Sigma
be an alphabet with
\mathit{Act}\cap(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}})=\emptyset
.
The
alphabet extension
of
\mathcal{U}
with respect to
\Sigma
is the rPA

\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma\rangle}=(S,{s^{\mathit{init}}},\mathit{Act}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}),\mathbf{P}_{\Sigma},L_{\Sigma})
over alphabet
\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}\cup\Sigma
, where

•

\mathbf{P}_{\Sigma}(s,\alpha)=\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
and
L_{\Sigma}(s,\alpha)=L(s,\alpha)
for all
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P})
and

•

\mathbf{P}_{\Sigma}(s,\mathsf{a})=\{\mathbf{1}_{s}\}
and
L_{\Sigma}(s,\mathsf{a})=\mathsf{a}
for all
s\in S
and
\mathsf{a}\in\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
.

Parallel composition for rPAs is defined analogously to the pPA composition in
Footnote

3
; the difference is that the transition function
\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}
is given by products of uncertainty sets rather than by products of single successor distributions.

{defi}
[Parallel Composition for rPAs]

For
i=1,2
, let
\mathcal{U}_{i}=(S_{i},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{i}},\mathit{Act}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i},L_{i})
be rPAs over alphabets
\Sigma_{i}
with
\mathit{Act}_{i}\cap(\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2})=\emptyset
.
The
parallel composition
of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
is given by the rPA

\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}=\big(S_{1}\times S_{2},({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}}),\mathit{Act}_{\parallel},\mathbf{P}_{\parallel},L_{\parallel}\big)

over
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
, where
\mathit{Act}_{\parallel}
and
L_{\parallel}
are defined as in
Footnote

3
and

•

for each
(s_{1},\alpha_{1})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{1})
,
(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{2})
with
L_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})=L_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\Sigma_{1}\cap\Sigma_{2}
:

\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{1},s_{2}),(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))=\{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}\mid\mu_{1}\in\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1}),\mu_{2}\in\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\},

•

for each
(s_{1},\alpha_{1})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{1})
,
s_{2}\in S_{2}
with
L_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})=\mathsf{a}_{1}\in\Sigma_{1}\setminus\Sigma_{2}
:

\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{1},s_{2}),(\alpha_{1},\mathsf{a}_{1}))=\{\mu_{1}\times\mathbf{1}_{s_{2}}\mid\mu_{1}\in\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})\},

•

for each
s_{1}\in S_{1}
,
(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{2})
with
L_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})=\mathsf{a}_{2}\in\Sigma_{2}\setminus\Sigma_{1}
:

\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{1},s_{2}),(\mathsf{a}_{2},\alpha_{2}))=\{\mathbf{1}_{s_{1}}\times\mu_{2}\mid\mu_{2}\in\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\}.

s_{0},t_{0}

s_{0},t_{1}

s_{1},t_{1}

s_{1},t_{2}

s_{0},t_{2}

s_{1},t_{0}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p\cdot q
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(1-p)\cdot q
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p\cdot(1-q)
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(1-p)\cdot(1-q)
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(1-q^{\prime})
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q^{\prime}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\frownie
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\frownie

Figure 6
.
Parallel composition
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
of the rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

5
.
The uncertainty sets in
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
are determined by the uncertainty sets of the individual components:
p\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
(from
\mathcal{U}_{1}
) and
q,q^{\prime}\in[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}]
(from
\mathcal{U}_{2}
).

{exa}

The parallel composition of the rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

5

is the rPA
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
depicted in
Figure

6
.

The uncertainty sets of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
are determined by the same interval bounds as in the components: for
\mathcal{U}_{1}
, the distributions in
\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{0},\mathsf{a})

are of the form
\mu_{1}(s_{0})=p,

\mu_{1}(s_{1})=1-p
for some
p\in[0,\frac{1}{2}],
and for
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, the distributions in
\mathbf{P}_{2}(t_{0},\mathsf{a})
are of the form
\mu_{2}(t_{1})=q,\mu_{2}(t_{2})=1-q
for some
q\in[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}].

In the composition, each uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{1},s_{2}),\cdot)
is obtained by taking products
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
of such component distributions.
Thus, the uncertainty sets of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
are described by the variables
p\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
q,q^{\prime}\in[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}]
, inherited from
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
.
When assuming memoryless nature, the rPA is equivalent to a corresponding pPA over parameters
p
,
q
and
q^{\prime}
and an appropriate region.
In our compositional setting, however, this correspondence is of limited use: memoryless nature may choose different distributions at different state–action pairs in the composition that share the same state of one component (e.g., at
(s_{0},t_{0})
and
(s_{1},t_{0})
), whereas in a pPA a valuation assigns a single global value to each parameter.

The following example shows that the parallel composition of polytopic (or convex) rPAs is, in general, not polytopic (or convex).
This has also been observed, e.g., by Schnitzer et al.
[
57
]
.

{exa}

Consider again the polytopic (convex) rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

5
and their parallel composition
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

6
.
We show that the uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))
is not convex, i.e.,

\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))\subsetneq\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))\right)
:

•

In
\mathcal{U}_{1}
, the uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{0},\mathsf{a})
contains distributions
\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathit{Dist}(\{s_{0},s_{1}\})
with
\mu_{1}(s_{0})=0,\mu_{1}(s_{1})=1,
and
\mu_{1}^{\prime}(s_{0})=\frac{1}{2}
,
\mu_{1}^{\prime}(s_{1})=\frac{1}{2}.

•

In
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, the uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}_{2}(t_{0},\mathsf{a})
contains distributions

\mu_{2},\mu_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathit{Dist}(\{t_{1},t_{2}\})
with
\mu_{2}(t_{1})=\frac{1}{10},\mu_{2}(t_{2})=\frac{9}{10},
and
\mu_{2}^{\prime}(t_{1})=\frac{9}{10},

\mu_{2}^{\prime}(t_{2})=\frac{1}{10}.

•

Hence the product distributions
\mu_{12}=\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{12}^{\prime}=\mu_{1}^{\prime}\times\mu_{2}^{\prime}
both belong to
\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))
.

•

However, the convex combination
\mu_{\mathrm{conv}}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\mu_{12}+\frac{3}{4}\cdot\mu_{12}^{\prime}\in\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))\right)
is not contained in
\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))
and thus the parallel composition is not polytopic (convex).

To see why
\mu_{\mathrm{conv}}\notin\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}((s_{0},t_{0}),(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))
, assume for contraposition that there exist
\hat{\mu}_{1}\in\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{0},\mathsf{a})
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}\in\mathbf{P}_{2}(t_{0},\mathsf{a})
such that
\mu_{\mathrm{conv}}=\hat{\mu}_{1}\times\hat{\mu}_{2}.

By the probabilities assigned by
\mu_{\mathrm{conv}}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{0})\cdot\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{1})

\displaystyle=\mu_{conv}((s_{0},t_{1}))=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\underbrace{\mu_{12}(s_{0},t_{1})}_{=0\cdot\frac{1}{10}}+\frac{3}{4}\cdot\underbrace{\mu_{12}^{\prime}(s_{0},t_{1})}_{=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{9}{10}}=\frac{27}{80}

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{0})\cdot\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu_{conv}((s_{0},t_{2}))=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\underbrace{\mu_{12}(s_{0},t_{2})}_{=0\cdot\frac{9}{10}}+\frac{3}{4}\cdot\underbrace{\mu_{12}^{\prime}(s_{0},t_{2})}_{=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{10}}=\frac{3}{80}

Both distributions are uniquely determined by

\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{0})
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{1})
, with

\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{1})=1-\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{0})
, and
\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{2})=1-\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{1}).

Together, this gives
\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{0})=\frac{3}{8}

and
\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{1})=\frac{9}{10}
.
However, this contradicts

\hat{\mu}_{1}(s_{1})\cdot\hat{\mu}_{2}(t_{1})=\frac{5}{8}\cdot\frac{9}{10}=\frac{9}{16}\neq\mu_{conv}((s_{1},t_{1}))=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\underbrace{\mu_{12}(s_{1},t_{1})}_{=1\cdot\frac{1}{10}}+\frac{3}{4}\cdot\underbrace{\mu_{12}^{\prime}(s_{1},t_{1})}_{=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{9}{10}}=\frac{29}{80}.

7.2.
Limitations for general rPAs

We investigate assume-guarantee reasoning for rPAs under parallel composition
\parallel
as in
Section

7.1
.
Our aim is to investigate whether the assume–guarantee framework of Kwiatkowska et al.
[
44
]
remains sound in the robust setting.
We focus on the asymmetric AG rule for (multi-objective) safety queries from

[
44
, Theorem 1]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}

(2)

where
A
and
G
are safety mo-queries with
\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{\textsf{G}}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}
.

We show that the above rule is not sound for general rPAs.
For
memoryless
nature (
\models\!{}_{\mathit{mless}}
), the rule already
fails for polytopic (and thus convex) rPAs; see
Section

7.2.1
.
For
memory-full
nature, the rule fails for
non-convex
rPAs;
see
Section

7.2.2
.
For the remaining cases—i.e., convex (including polytopic) rPAs with memory-full nature—we can recover AG reasoning proof rules as outlined in
Section

7.3
.

7.2.1.
Memoryless nature

We show that the asymmetric AG proof rule from (
7.3
) is not sound when nature is restricted to be memoryless, i.e., using
\models\!{}_{\mathit{mless}}
, even for polytopic (convex) rPAs.

{exa}

Consider the polytopic rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

5
; both are iPAs (and thus polytopic rPAs).
Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
be their parallel
composition as in
Figure

6
(which is not a polytopic rPA; see
Section

7.1
).

We instantiate the asymmetric AG rule with the following safety mo-queries:

\textsf{A}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}\!(\mathcal{L}_{A}),
where
\mathcal{L}_{A}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}^{\infty}
, i.e.,
A
is trivially satisfied by
\mathcal{U}_{1}
, and
\textsf{G}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0.25}\!(\mathcal{L}_{G}),
where

\mathcal{L}_{G}=\{w\in\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c},\frownie\}^{\infty}\mid w\text{ does not start with }\mathsf{a}\mathsf{c}\mathsf{a}\frownie\}.

Under memoryless-nature semantics, we have:

•

\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}_{\mathit{mless}}\textsf{A}
, since
A
is
trivially true, and

•

{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}_{\mathit{mless}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}
, since in
\mathcal{U}_{2},
the maximal probability—among all memoryless natures and partial strategies—of starting with the prefix
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{c}\mathsf{a}\frownie
is
0.25
, which implies that
G
is satisfied.
In particular, for any memoryless nature
\nu
and (partial) strategy
\sigma
of
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}
, if
A
holds under
\nu
and
\sigma
, then so does
G
.

Thus both premises of the asymmetric rule (
7.3
) are satisfied under
\models\!{}_{\mathit{mless}}
.
However, there exists a complete strategy
\sigma
and a memoryless nature
\nu
such that
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2},\nu,\sigma\not\models{}\!\textsf{G}
:

•

\sigma
selects with probability 1
the action with label
\frownie
in
(s_{1},t_{2})
and the unique available action in the remaining states.

•

\nu
chooses in
(s_{0},t_{0})
after action with label
\mathsf{a}
a distribution
\mu
with
\mu((s_{1},t_{1}))=\frac{9}{10}
,

\mu((s_{1},t_{2}))=\frac{1}{10}
, and
\mu((s_{0},t_{i}))=0
, for
i\in{1,2}
.
In
(s_{1},t_{0})
after seeing label
\mathsf{a}
, nature chooses a distribution
\mu^{\prime}
with
\mu^{\prime}((s_{1},t_{2}))=\frac{9}{10}
and
\mu^{\prime}((s_{1},t_{1}))=\frac{1}{10}
.

For this strategy and nature, the probability of violating
\mathcal{L}_{G}
(i.e., starting with the bad prefix
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{c}\mathsf{a}\frownie
) is
\frac{81}{100}
.
Hence, the probability of satisfying
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is strictly less than
0.25
, i.e.,
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}\not\models^{\mathit{cmp}}_{\mathit{mless}}\textsf{G}.

Intuitively, the problem is that a memoryless nature in
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, must fix a single distribution for each state–action pair (in particular for
t_{0}
in
\mathcal{U}_{2}
),
whereas in the composition
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
the state–action pairs
(s_{0},t_{0})
and

(s_{1},t_{0})
are distinct. Nature may choose different distributions at these two composite states, which yields behaviours that cannot be captured by a single memoryless nature on the component
\mathcal{U}_{2}
.

To summarize, the asymmetric AG rule is not sound under memoryless nature, even when restricted to convex (or polytopic) rPAs. In fact, the above example also shows that the memoryless-nature AG rule fails for convex rPAs even when using the convexity-preserving composition
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
introduced in
Section

7.3
.

7.2.2.
Non-convex rPAs

We now consider the asymmetric AG rule (
7.3
) for rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
whose uncertainty sets are not assumed to be convex, under memory-full nature.
In this setting, the reduction to PAs no longer preserves behaviour in general, and the PA-based AG framework does not carry over as illustrated by the following example.

{exa}

Reconsider the rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
depicted in
Figure

5
.
We define
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
by modifying the transition uncertainty functions as follows:

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}}(s_{0},\mathsf{a})=\{\mu_{1}\colon s_{0},s_{1}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\}

and

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}}(t_{0},\mathsf{a})=\{\mu_{2,1}\colon t_{1}\mapsto\frac{9}{10},t_{2}\mapsto\frac{1}{10}\,,~\mu_{2,2}\colon t_{1}=\frac{1}{10},t_{2}=\frac{9}{10}\}
.
The remaining transitions are as in
Figure

5
.

\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
is not convex.
The parallel composition
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
is the rPA in
Figure

6
, where the uncertainty sets in
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
are now determined by
p=\frac{1}{2}
(from
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}
) and
q,q^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}\}
(from
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
).

We instantiate the asymmetric AG rule with the following safety mo-queries:

\textsf{A}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0.4}\!(\mathcal{L}),
and
\textsf{G}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0.8}\!(\mathcal{L}),
where
\mathcal{L}=\{w\in\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}^{\infty}\mid w\text{ does not start with }\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}\}.

We have:

•

\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}
,
since under the unique strategy and nature of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}
the prefix
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}
is seen with probability 0.5,
and

•

{\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}_{\mathit{mless}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}
, since, in order for the assumption
A
to hold in
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
, any nature must resolve the uncertainty at
t_{0}
after taking the action labeled
\mathsf{a}
by choosing the distribution with
\mu(t_{1})=\frac{9}{10}
(rather than
\frac{1}{10}
).
Under this choice, the probability of producing the prefix
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}
is at most
0.1
, so
G
is satisfied.

Both premises of the asymmetric rule are satisfied.
However,
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime},\nu,\sigma\not\models{}\!\textsf{G}
, for the following complete strategy
\sigma
and nature
\nu
:

•

strategy
\sigma
selects the action with label
\mathsf{a}
in state
(s_{0},t_{0})
, and the action with label
\mathsf{b}
in
(s_{1},t_{2})
, all with probability 1.

•

nature
\nu
chooses a distribution
\mu
with
\mu((s_{1},t_{2}))=\frac{9}{20}
in
(s_{0},t_{0})
after seeing label
\mathsf{a}
.

For this strategy
\sigma
and nature
\nu
, the probability that the trace starts with
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}
is
\frac{9}{20}=0.45
, i.e.,
\sigma
and
\nu
witness violation of
\textsf{G}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0.8}\!(\mathcal{L})
.

Note that for the convex rPA
\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

5(b)
, the second premise fails:

{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}
does not hold.
For example, in state
t_{0}
, after the strategy has chosen the action labeled
\mathsf{a}
, nature can select a distribution
\mu
with
\mu(t_{1})=\frac{7}{10}
and
\mu(t_{2})=\frac{3}{10}
.
Under this choice, the assumption
A
is satisfied, but the guarantee
G
with threshold
0.8
is violated.

For PAs, the proof of the asymmetric AG rule in
[
44
]
relies on the strategies’ ability to randomise over successor distributions in order to define strategy projections (see
Section

3.1
).
In the rPA setting, nature is non-probabilistic.
For non-convex uncertainty sets
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
, it might not be possible to adequatly project all natures of a composed rPA to natures of its individual components—preventing compositional reasoning for general rPAs.

7.3.
Compositionality of convex rPAs with memory-full nature

For rPAs with
convex
uncertainty sets, probabilistic extensions of the nature formalism are as expressive as non-probabilistic variants: any distribution
\tilde{\mu}\in\mathit{Dist}(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha))
over distributions from an uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
can be flattened into a distribution
\mu\in\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)=\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
.
Intuitively, once probabilistic and memory-full natures can be assumed,
the conceptual difference between (r)PA strategies and rPA natures vanishes.

We define a
PA-reduction
that transforms a convex rPA under memory-full nature to an equivalent (possibly uncountably branching) PA by lifting nature choices to strategy choices.
The construction is analogous to a standard reduction from polytopic rMDPs
[
50
,
66
,
33
,
3
]
to stochastic games.
Let the
generator
of a convex uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
be given by

\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)~=~\begin{cases}\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)&\text{if }\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)=\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)\right)\\
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We have
\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)\right)=\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
for any convex
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
.
The set
\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)
is finite iff
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
is polytopic.
If
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
is an open set, we have
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\neq\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)\right)
and
\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)=\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)
is uncountable infinite.

{defi}
[PA-Reduction for rPAs]

Let
\mathcal{U}=\left(S,{s^{\mathit{init}}},\mathit{Act},\mathbf{P},L\right){}
be a convex rPA.
The
PA-reduction
of a convex rPA
\mathcal{U}=\left(S,{s^{\mathit{init}}},\mathit{Act},\mathbf{P},L\right){}
is the PA
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)=\left(S,{s^{\mathit{init}}},\mathit{Act}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)},\mathbf{P}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)},L_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}\right)
, where

\mathit{Act}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}=\{(\alpha,\mu)\mid\exists(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}):\mu\in\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)\}
is the (possibly uncountable) set of actions and for each
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P})
and each
\mu\in\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)
, we set

\mathbf{P}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}(s,(\alpha,\mu))=\mu
and

L_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}(s,(\alpha,\mu))=L(s,\alpha).

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
is a finite-state, finite-action PA iff
\mathcal{U}
is polytopic.
There is a correspondence between strategy-nature pairs
(\sigma,\nu)
of rPA
\mathcal{U}
and strategies
\widehat{\sigma}
of
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
yielding a semantic equivalence:
\mathcal{U}
(under memor-full nature) and
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
satisfy the same objectives, multi-objective properties, and AG triples.
Further details in
Section

A.6
.

For the remainder of this section, we fix two convex rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
with alphabets
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, respectively.
Our observations so far enable a reduction to AG reasoning for (standard) PAs.
For example, when combining the asymmetric AG rule of
[
44
]
with the above-mentioned equivalences of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
and
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{i}\right)
for
i=1,2
, we obtain the rule:

\displaystyle\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}

To reason about the composed rPA
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
, it remains to relate it to the PA
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
.
However, the two composed models are not equivalent in general:
the randomised strategy choices of the PA
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
are inherently convex whereas parallel composition of rPAs does not preserve convexity (cf.
Section

7.1
).
The latter further prevents compositional reasoning with more than two convex rPAs.
We therefore introduce
convex parallel composition

\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
.
Given
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
, the rPA
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
is obtained by replacing, for each state–action pair, the uncertainty set of the composition by its convex hull.

{defi}
[Convex Parallel Composition for rPAs]

Given rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, let
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}=(S_{1}\times S_{2},({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}}),\mathit{Act}_{\parallel},\mathbf{P}_{\parallel},L_{\parallel})
be as in
Section

7.1
.
The
convex parallel composition
of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
is the rPA

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}=(S_{1}\times S_{2},({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}}),\mathit{Act}_{\parallel},\mathbf{P}_{\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}},L_{\parallel})
,
where
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}}(s,\alpha)=\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}(s,\alpha)\right)
for all
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\parallel})
.

Convex parallel composition is associative, in the sense that

(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2})\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{3}
and

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}(\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{3})
are equivalent up to renaming of states.
We therefore write
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{3}
without parentheses.
Every uncertainty set of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}
is a subset of an uncertainty set of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
.
Hence,
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
over-approximates the standard composition
\parallel
and any (multi-objective) property/AG triple satisfied by
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\ldots\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{n}
immediately transfers to the
standard parallel composition
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\ldots\parallel\mathcal{U}_{n}
.
The following lemma shows that the PA reduction commutes with the convex parallel composition w.r.t. satisfaction of multi-objective queries.

Lemma 23
.

Let
\star\in\{\mathit{prt},\mathit{cmp}\}
and let
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
be convex rPAs.
Then, for any mo-query
X
over
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
:

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\star}\textsf{X}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\star}\textsf{X}

We derive AG rules for convex rPAs under the convex parallel composition
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
, focusing on safety multi-objective queries.
6
6

6

Extensions to fairness and more general quantitative objectives require a careful treatment of nature in order to preserve fairness properties under the reduction to PAs and are left for future work.

Full proofs are given in
Section

A.6
.
We lift the asymmetric AG rule from
13
(cf.
[
44
, Theorem 1]
) and the circular AG rule
[
44
, Theorem 5]
to convex rPAs.

Theorem 24

(Asymmetric and Circular Rule)
.

Let
A
and
G
be safety
mo-queries over alphabets

\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
and

\Sigma_{\textsf{G}}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}
, respectively.
Then the following proof rules hold:

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}_{1}{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}{\mathcal{U}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}_{2}}

Proof 7.1

(Proof sketch)
.

Following the explanations above, the proof is based on a reduction to the PA setting of
[
44
]
.
We reduce convex rPAs to (possibly uncountable branching) PAs (see
Section

7.3
).
Under this reduction, the premises for
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
induce the corresponding premises required by the asymmetric (circular) AG rule for PAs from
[
44
]
, i.e.,
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)
and
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
(see
37
), yielding
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}
.
By
23
follows that
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\star}\textsf{G}
.
Then, we lift this back to convex rPAs (see
39
), obtaining
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}
.

As observed above,
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
over-approximates
\parallel
.
Consequently, if
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}
,
then also
\mathcal{U}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}
.
The remaining AG rules from
13
and
4
can be lifted to convex rPAs in a similar manner; see
Appendix

D
for details.

Remark 25
.

The premises of the asymmetric and the circular rule can be verified directly on the PA-reduction.
This is particularly useful for polytopic rPAs, where the PA-reduction is finite
(see, e.g.,
[
50
,
66
,
33
,
64
,
3
]
).

7.4.
Interval PAs

Interval probabilistic automata
(iPAs) are a special case of convex rPAs, in which each uncertainty set is specified by interval bounds on the transition probabilities.
We lift the iMDP definition of Badings
[
3
, Definition 3.27]
to the
compositional setting:

{defi}

An
interval probabilistic automaton
(iPA) over finite alphabet
\Sigma

is an rPA
\mathcal{I}=\left(S,{s^{\mathit{init}}},\mathit{Act},\mathbf{P},L\right){}
as in
Section

7.1
, such that for all
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P})
there exist
\ell^{(s,\alpha,s^{\prime})},u^{(s,\alpha,s^{\prime})}\in[0,1]\text{ for each }s^{\prime}\in S
such that

\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)~=~\big\{\mu\in\mathit{Dist}(S)~\big|\forall s^{\prime}\in S:\ell^{(s,\alpha,s^{\prime})}\leq\mu(s^{\prime})\leq u^{(s,\alpha,s^{\prime})}\big\}.

{exa}

The rPAs in
Figure

5
—described in
Section

7.1
—are iPAs.
Each transition annotated with an interval
[\ell,u]
represents the uncertainty set of all distributions whose probability for the corresponding successor lies between
\ell
and
u
. Their parallel composition—depicted in
Figures

7
and
7.1
—is not an iPA.

The class of iPAs is neither closed under
\parallel
nor
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
.
The literature (e.g.,
[
46
]
) often employs an interval-arithmetic relaxation
\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}
of the parallel composition.
This relaxation preserves the iPA property—
\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{I}_{2}
is again an iPA—but introduces spurious distributions
[
57
]
.
We adopt this relaxation to our setting, and show by example that the AG framework of
[
44
]
—precisely the asymmetric rule—cannot be lifted directly to this relaxed composition.

{defi}

[Interval-relaxation Composition]Given iPAs
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{I}_{2}=(S_{1}\times S_{2},({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}}),\mathit{Act}_{\parallel},\mathbf{P}_{\parallel},L_{\parallel})
be their parallel composition rPA as in
Section

7.1
.
The
interval-arithmetic parallel composition
of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
is the iPA

\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{I}_{2}=(S_{1}\times S_{2},({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}}),\mathit{Act}_{\parallel},\mathbf{P}_{\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}},L_{\parallel})
,
where for all
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\parallel})
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}}(s,\alpha)~=~\Big\{\mu\in\mathit{Dist}(S_{1}\times S_{2})~\Big|

\displaystyle~\forall s^{\prime}=(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in S_{1}\times S_{2}\colon

\displaystyle~\min_{\mu_{\parallel}\in\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}(s,\alpha)}\mu_{\parallel}(s^{\prime})~\leq~\mu(s)~\leq~\max_{\mu_{\parallel}\in\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}(s,\alpha)}\mu_{\parallel}(s^{\prime})\Big\}.

s_{0},t_{0}

s_{0},t_{1}

s_{1},t_{1}

s_{1},t_{2}

s_{0},t_{2}

s_{1},t_{0}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[0,\frac{9}{20}]
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{20},\frac{9}{10}]
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[0,\frac{9}{20}]
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{20},\frac{9}{10}]
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}]
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}[\frac{1}{10},\frac{9}{10}]
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\frownie
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\frownie

Figure 7
.
Interval-arithmetic relaxation
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
of the iPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
from
Figure

5
.

{exa}

Figure

7
shows the interval-arithmetic relaxation composition

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
of the iPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}

from
Figure

5
, synchronising on their common labels
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{b}
.

The asymmetric AG rule for safety (multi-objective) queries from
[
44
, Theorem 1]
, instantiated in our setting for iPAs under
\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}
, would read as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{I}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}{\mathcal{I}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\mathcal{I}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}

(3)

This AG rule is not sound for iPAs under composition
\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}
, as there exist counterexamples for both memoryless and memory-full nature, see
Section

7.4
.

{exa}

For
\mathcal{U}_{1}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
as in
Section

7.4
, consider the safety multi-objective queries

\textsf{A}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0}\!(\mathcal{L}_{A})
, for
\mathcal{L}_{A}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}^{\infty},
(which is trivially satisfied) and

\textsf{G}=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0.1}\!(\mathcal{L}_{G}),
for
\mathcal{L}_{G}=\{w\in\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c},\frownie\}^{\infty}\mid w\text{ does not contain any }\mathsf{c}\}
.
The premises of the AG rule are satisfied for
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
:

•

\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}
, since
\mathcal{L}_{A}
contains
all words over
\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}
,

•

{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}
holds, i.e., for

\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}
any (partial) strategy and nature satisfying
A
also satisfies

G
.

However, there exists a complete strategy
\sigma
and a nature
\nu
such that
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{U}_{2},\nu,\sigma\not\models{}\!\textsf{G}
:

•

\sigma
always selects the action with label
\mathsf{a}
in state
(s_{0},t_{0})
,
and, upon reaching a state with second component
t_{1}
, always selects the
action with label
\mathsf{c}
.

•

(memoryless) nature
\nu
chooses at
(s_{0},t_{0}),\mathsf{a}
the distribution

\mu\colon(s_{0},t_{1})\mapsto\frac{1}{20},(s_{1},t_{1})\mapsto\frac{9}{10},(s_{1},t_{2})\mapsto\frac{1}{20}
.

Then, the probability of eventually seeing the label
\mathsf{c}
is
\frac{19}{20}
, so the probability of satisfying
\mathcal{L}_{G}
(i.e., of never seeing
\mathsf{c}
) is
\frac{1}{20}<0.1
.
Hence,
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\not\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

Intuitively, the interval relaxation
\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}
discards all dependencies between successor state probabilities which allows for uncertainty resolutions that can not be projected to the individual components.
As a consequence, the AG framework of
[
44
]
cannot be applied directly to iPAs under the relaxed composition
\mathbin{\parallel^{iRel}}
.
Since nature in the counterexample above is memoryless, the rule is also unsound under the memoryless semantics
\models\!{}_{\mathit{mless}}
.

8.
Simulation-Based Assume-Guarantee Reasoning for pPAs

We develop a sound and complete AG proof rule for pPAs based on a
simulation relation
, following the work of Komuravelli et al.
[
42
]
for PAs, which builds upon the simulation-based AG frameworks of
[
5
,
52
]
.
While the quantitative AG framework of Kwiatkowska et al.
[
44
]
considered so far focuses on multi-objective properties, the simulation-based approach is
model-based
: assumptions and guarantees are given as PAs, and reasoning proceeds via a simulation relation between the automata rather than via property satisfaction.

Concretely, the approach of
[
42
]
employs strong simulation
[
58
]
(denoted
\preceq
) and establishes the following asymmetric AG rule for PAs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{A}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{A}

Here,
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
are system components,
\mathcal{N}_{A}
is an assumption on the environment of
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{G}
models the desired behaviour of the overall system, i.e., of the composition.
The conclusion
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}
ensures that
\mathcal{N}_{G}
strongly simulates the composite system.
Preservation of specifications is then obtained indirectly from the fact that strong simulation preserves a suitable logic fragment, e.g., a safety fragment of PCTL
[
10
]
; that is, if
\mathcal{N}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{\mathit{spec}}
holds and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathit{spec}}\models\Psi
, then also
\mathcal{N}\models\Psi
.

We lift simulation-based AG reasoning to pPAs.
The asymmetric rule depends on two properties of the simulation relation:

{enumerate*}

it is a
preorder
, i.e., reflexive and transitive, and

it is
compositional
with respect to parallel composition.

We introduce two variants of simulation relations for pPAs—
strong simulation
and
robust-strong simulation
—which satisfy (i) and (ii).
Classical strong simulation for PAs
[
58
]
is lifted to the pPA setting by defining simulation with respect to a region of valuations and thus capturing parameter dependencies across components.
The main result of this section is a simulation-based AG proof rule for pPAs (cf.
32
).

8.1.
AG reasoning via strong simulation for PAs

We first recall strong simulation for PAs as introduced by Segala and Lynch
[
58
]
and the asymmetric simulation-based AG rule of Komuravelli et al.
[
42
]
.
For this section, let
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\left(S_{i},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{i}},\mathit{Act}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i},L_{i}\right)
over alphabet
\Sigma_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
be two PAs.

For non-probabilistic labeled transition systems, simulation is defined in terms of successor states: a state
s_{2}
simulates a state
s_{1}
if, for each successor of
s_{1}
, there is a matching successor of
s_{2}
that again simulates it.
In the probabilistic setting, transitions lead to
distributions
over successor states, and the simulation relation has to be lifted from states to distributions.
Segala and Lynch formalise this by means of weight functions
[
58
, Definition 15]
.
We use an equivalent characterisation that is given in
[
68
, Lemma 4.2.1]
:

{defi}

For relation
\varrho{}\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
and
\mu_{i}\in\mathit{Dist}(S_{i}){}
(
i=1,2
),
let
\mu_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\varrho{}}\mu_{2}
iff for every
A\subseteq S_{1}
we have
\mu_{1}(A)\leq\mu_{2}(\varrho(A)),
where
\varrho(A)=\{s\in S_{2}\mid\exists s^{\prime}\in A:(s^{\prime},s)\in\varrho{}\}
.

Intuitively,
\mu_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\varrho{}}\mu_{2}
means that for every subset
A
of the domain of
\mu_{1}
, the probability mass that
\mu_{1}
assigns to
A
does not exceed the mass that
\mu_{2}
assigns to the set of states related to
A
via
\varrho{}
.

{defi}

A
strong simulation
between
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
is a relation
\varrho\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
such that

({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}})\in\varrho
and
for every
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\varrho
and

(s_{1},\alpha_{1})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{1})
, there is

(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{2})

such that
L_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})=L_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})

and
\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})\sqsubseteq_{\varrho}\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})
.

\mathcal{N}_{2}
strongly simulates
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, written
\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{2}
, iff there is a strong simulation between
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
.

Remark 26
.

Strong simulation preserves satisfaction of safety PCTL formulas
[
10
, Lemma 2.11]
:
if
\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{2}
, then for every safety PCTL formula
\Psi
—for example

\Psi=\mathbb{P}_{\geq 0.9}\!(\Box\frownie)
—we have that

\mathcal{N}_{2}\models\Psi
implies
\mathcal{N}_{1}\models\Psi
.

Lemma 27

(
[
58
, Proposition 17]
)
.

Strong simulation
\preceq
is a preorder, i.e., reflexive and transitive. Moreover, if
\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
, then for every PA
\mathcal{N}
:
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}
.

Theorem 28

(
[
T
]
heorem 1)
.

Kom+12]

The simulation-based AG rule is sound and complete:

\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{A}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{A}

Proof 8.1
.

Towards completeness, it has to be shown that whenever
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}
holds, there exists an assumption PA
\mathcal{N}_{A}
such that both premises are satisfied—which follows directly by instantiating the assumption with
\mathcal{N}_{A}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
and by commutativity of
\parallel
.

For soundness, assume the premises hold.
From
\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{A}
and
\Sigma_{A}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
, compositionality of strong simulation (
27
) yields

\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}~\preceq~\mathcal{N}_{A}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}~=~\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{A}.

The second premise
\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{A}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}
and transitivity of
\preceq
then imply
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{G}.

Intuitively,
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
are system components,
\mathcal{N}_{A}
is an assumption on the environment of
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{G}
is a guarantee specification for the composite system.
The rule states that if
\mathcal{N}_{1}
conforms (via strong simulation) to the assumption
\mathcal{N}_{A}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{A}
simulates the guarantee
\mathcal{N}_{G}
, then the actual composition
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}
also conforms to
\mathcal{N}_{G}
.
Via
26
, the guarantee
\mathcal{N}_{G}
can be given as a safety PCTL formula
\Psi
: if
\mathcal{N}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{N}_{A}
and
(\mathcal{N}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{A})\models\Psi
for some safety PCTL formula
\Psi
, then
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\models\Psi
as well.

8.2.
Strong and robust-strong simulation for pPAs

We lift strong simulation from PAs to pPAs by parameterising the simulation relation with a region of valuations.
We consider two variants of simulation for pPAs:
strong simulation
and a stricter
robust-strong simulation
.
The former allows the witnessing simulation relation to depend on the chosen valuation, whereas the latter requires a single relation that works uniformly for all valuations in the region.
We then show that both relations are preorders and compositional.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\left(S_{i},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{i}},V_{i},\mathit{Act}_{i},\mathbf{P}_{i},L_{i}\right)
for
i=1,2
be two pPAs over
\Sigma_{i}
and let
{R}
be a region that is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

{defi}

\mathcal{M}_{2}

strongly simulates

\mathcal{M}_{1}
w.r.t. region
{R}
, written
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
, iff

\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]

for all valuations
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
.

s_{0}

s_{1}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\frac{9}{10}}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\frac{1}{10}}

(a)

(p)PA
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}

t_{0}

t_{1}

t_{2}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1-p
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1-p

(b)

pPA
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}

Figure 8
.
Example pPAs
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}
.

{exa}

Consider the pPAs
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}
in
Figure

8
.
Let
{R}=\{\mathpzc{v}:\{p\}\to\mathbb{R}\mid\mathpzc{v}(p)\in\{\tfrac{1}{10},\tfrac{9}{10}\}\}.

For the valuation
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
with
\mathpzc{v}(p)=\tfrac{1}{10}
, the relation

\varrho_{1}=\{(s_{0},t_{0}),(s_{1},t_{1})\}
is a strong simulation (in the sense of
Section

8.1
) between the instantiated PAs
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}]
.
For the valuation
\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}\in{R}
with
\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}(p)=\tfrac{9}{10}
, the relation
\varrho_{2}=\{(s_{0},t_{0}),(s_{1},t_{2})\}
is a strong simulation between the instantiated PAs
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}]
.
Hence,
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}.

As the example shows, the simulation relations witnessing
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
generally depend on the valuation.
We introduce a stricter notion of simulation between two pPAs that requires a
single
relation to witness strong simulation for all valuations in the region.

{defi}

A relation
\varrho\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
is a
robust-strong simulation
between
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
w.r.t.
{R}
if, for all valuations
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
, the relation
\varrho
is a strong simulation (in the sense of
Section

8.1
) between the instantiated PAs
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]
.

The pPA
\mathcal{M}_{2}

robust-strongly simulates

\mathcal{M}_{1}
w.r.t.
{R}
, written
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}
, iff there is a robust-strong simulation between
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

Corollary 29
.

\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}

implies

\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
. The converse does not hold.

Remark 30
.

Strong simulation for pPAs preserves safety PCTL formulas, similar to strong simulation for PAs.
More precisely, for a safety PCTL formula
\Psi
, if
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
, then for all valuations
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]\models\Psi
implies
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\models\Psi.

For the robust-strong simulation relation
\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}
, we have the following result:
If
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\Psi
is a safety PCTL formula, then

\mathcal{M}_{2},{R}\models\Psi
implies
\mathcal{M}_{1},{R}\models\Psi.

{exa}

Consider again the pPAs
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}
from
Figure

8
and the region
{R}=\{\mathpzc{v}:\{p\}\to\mathbb{R}\mid\mathpzc{v}(p)\in\{\tfrac{1}{10},\tfrac{9}{10}\}\}
.
As shown in
Section

8.2
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}
, since for each
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
there exists a strong simulation between
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}]
.
The witnessing relations
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
, however, depend on the valuation
\mathpzc{v}
: there is no single relation
\varrho
that is a strong simulation in the sense of
Section

8.1
for all
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\not\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Now we define a new pPA
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
from
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}
by changing the
\mathsf{b}
-labeled transition from
s_{1}
so that its successor distribution is
\mu
with
\mu(s_{0})=p
and
\mu(s_{1})=1-p
.
Then, the relation
\varrho=\{(s_{0},t_{0}),(s_{1},t_{1})\}
is a strong simulation between
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime\prime}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}[\mathpzc{v}]
for every
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
.
We therefore obtain
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 31
.

Let
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}}\in\{\preceq_{{R}},\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\}
.
The relation
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}}
is a
preorder
and
compositional
: if
\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
, then for every pPA
\mathcal{M}
for which
{R}
is well-defined, we have
\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M}
.

The proof of
31
is given in
Section

A.5
.
Using
31
, we can lift the AG rule from
28
to the parametric setting and obtain the following asymmetric AG rule for pPAs.

Theorem 32
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{A}
,
\mathcal{M}_{G}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
be pPAs with
\Sigma_{A}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
, and let
{R}_{1}
and
{R}_{2}
be well-defined regions.
Let
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}\in\{\preceq,\preceq^{robust}\}
.
The following rule is sound and complete:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\mathcal{M}_{G}\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{A}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{G}\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{A}

Proof 8.2

(Proof Sketch)
.

Completeness follows as in the PA case by instantiating
\mathcal{M}_{A}
with
\mathcal{M}_{1}
; soundness relies on
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}}
being a preorder and compositional (
31
).
The full proof is given in
Section

A.5
.

9.
Related Work

Compositional verification has been widely studied in both probabilistic and non-probabilistic systems.
We summarize the key contributions related to our work.

AG reasoning.

Jones’ rely-guarantee method
[
34
]
and Pnueli’s compositional proof system
[
55
]
for linear temporal logic laid the foundation for AG reasoning.
Subsequent works focused on AG rules for systems using CTL
∗

[
14
]
and developed AG reasoning for
reactive modules

[
32
,
1
]
.
Automated AG reasoning techniques include learning-based assumption generation
[
15
,
52
]
.
More recent work has focused on circular AG reasoning
[
18
]
and verification-repair approaches
[
24
]
.

AG reasoning for probabilistic systems.

The initial work by de Alfaro et al.
[
17
]
introduced AG rules for a probabilistic extension of reactive modules
[
32
,
1
]
.
Their model is similar to PA
[
58
,
59
]
, but limited to synchronous parallel composition.
Kwiatkowska et al.
[
43
,
23
]
generalized AG verification for PA, allowing more flexible parallel composition and extending AG reasoning to probabilistic safety properties.
Their approach reduces AG verification to multi-objective model checking, as proposed by Etessami et al.
[
19
]
.
This line of work was further refined in
[
44
]
, enabling AG reasoning over a broader class of quantitative properties, including conjunctions over probabilistic liveness and expected rewards.
Algorithmic learning-based assumption generation techniques
[
15
,
26
]
were later adapted for AG reasoning in probabilistic settings
[
20
,
21
,
47
]
.
Other assumption generation approaches include abstraction-refinement methods
[
42
,
12
]
, based on the CEGAR paradigm
[
13
]
, and symbolic learning-based methods
[
31
]
.
Hampus and Nyberg
[
29
]
develop a theory of assume‑guarantee contracts for probabilistic cyber‑physical systems, supporting both probabilistic and non‑probabilistic behaviour, together with a deductive system to prove probabilistic properties.

AG reasoning for models with uncertainty.

The notion of bisimulation for parametric Markov models studied by Hahn et al.
[
28
]
is closely related to our robust‑strong simulation: both require a single relation that constitutes a strong simulation (in the PA sense) for every valuation in a region.
Bouchekir and Boukala
[
6
]
propose a learning‑based symbolic AG framework
for MDPs where assumptions are expressed as interval MDPs encoded with MTBDDs (multi-terminal BDDs).
Compositional reasoning for interval MDPs via bisimulation has been investigated by Hashemi et al.
[
30
]
.

Sequential composition.

Another recent line of research focuses on compositional reasoning of
sequentially
composed MDPs:
Junges and Spaan
[
39
]
introduced an abstraction-refinement approach for hierarchical probabilistic models, leveraging parametric MDPs to represent sets of similar subroutines.
Watanabe et al.
[
65
]
exploit string diagrams to verifying sequentially composed MDPs.
Multi-objective reachability properties are a central element in this approach.

Our contribution.

We extend AG reasoning to parametric and robust PAs (and sub-classes thereof), leveraging research on parametric MDPs
[
40
,
56
,
37
]
, robust MDPs
[
3
,
50
,
66
,
33
,
64
]
, and two approach to AG verification for PAs
[
44
,
42
]
.
Our framework enables to reason about monotonicity
[
62
,
60
,
61
]
in a compositional manner.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first AG-based compositional verification framework for
parametric
and
robust
PAs.

10.
Conclusion

{forest}

Figure 9
.
Overview of established AG rules for pPAs and rPAs.

Summary

We presented an assume-guarantee framework for the compositional verification of Segala’s probabilistic automata (PA) extended with uncertainty.
We studied various forms of uncertainty ranging from parameters to uncertainty sets.
Parametric PAs allow for modeling dependencies between probabilistic branching at different states; a valuation fixes all parameters once and for all.
After selecting an action in robust PA, nature selects a distribution from the uncertainty set.
An overview of our established results is provided in
Figure

9
.

Our results are primarily generalisations of the AG rules for PAs by Kwiatkowska et al.
[
44
]
.
Our AG rules for pPAs cover safety and
\omega
-regular properties as well as (parametric) expected total rewards and their multi-objective combinations.
We also introduced compositional proof rules to reason about monotonicity.
For the robust setting, we studied AG rules for different forms of nature, and for different sub-classes of robust PAs, such as convex and interval PAs.
We showed that the asymmetric AG rule of
[
44
]
can be lifted to convex rPAs with history-dependent nature under a convexity-preserving parallel composition operator. The majority of our results for robust PA are negative though: the asymmetric AG rule is neither applicable to memoryless nature, non-convex uncertainty sets, nor the interval relaxation of parallel composition.

Finally, we developed an alternative, simulation-based AG framework for parametric PAs in the style of Komuravelli et al.
[
42
]
, introducing strong and robust-strong simulation preorders and establishing sound and complete asymmetric AG rules.

These contributions lay the theoretical foundations for modular verification of parametric PAs and robust PAs.

Future work

There are many different directions for future work.
An implementation can demonstrate the effectiveness of AG reasoning.
This requires efficient multi-objective verification algorithms for pMDPs and rMDPs, in particular to verify the AG premises.
Existing techniques for single-objective parametric MDPs
[
37
]
provide a good starting point, as well as robust multi-objective verification for interval MDPs
[
27
]
.
Extensions towards other properties such as long-run average rewards and expected visiting times
[
48
]
, as well as investigating other models, such as partially observable MDPs (see also the preliminary works
[
9
,
67
,
69
]
) are of interest.
For robust PAs, additional nature semantics (e.g., Markovian or at-every-step nature
[
2
]
, see
21
) as well as fairness assumptions could be investigated.

Another direction is to study algorithmic verification of strong and robust-strong simulation for pPAs, and to develop AG rules based on alternative simulation preorders, such as weak and probabilistic simulations
[
58
]
. Additionally, circular AG proof rules for pPAs, following the approach of Elkader et al.
[
18
]
, could be studied.
Extensions of the simulation-based approach to robust PAs and interval PAs could be inspired by (bi)simulation notions for robust and interval models
[
35
,
36
,
30
]
.

We plan to investigate whether the assume-guarantee framework can be lifted to interleaved probabilistic I/O automata with distributed schedulers
[
25
]
, where partial-information constraints lead to undecidability of reachability and model-checking problems.
Finally, CEGAR-style and learning-based assumption generation techniques for parametric and robust PAs are of interest, following e.g., the ideas of
[
42
,
5
,
52
,
11
,
21
,
6
]
.

, and

s_{2}\in S_{2}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}_{\parallel}\big(\mathit{last}(\pi),(\hat{\alpha}_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2}),(s_{1},s_{2})\big)}^{=1}.

See
4

Proof A.2
.

We show the statement by induction over the length of a path
\pi_{i}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\mathit{fin}}
.
For
|\pi_{i}|=0
, i.e.,
\pi_{i}={s^{\mathit{init}}_{i}}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\mathit{fin}}
, we have
Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}}}\!\left({s^{\mathit{init}}_{i}}\right)=1=Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left({s^{\mathit{init}}_{i}}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)
.
For the induction step, assume that the statement holds for
\pi_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\mathit{fin}}
and consider
\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}^{\prime},\alpha_{i},s_{i}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\mathit{fin}}
.
If
Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)=0
, the induction hypothesis implies
Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}_{i}\right)=0
, yielding
Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}}\!\left(\pi_{i}\right)=0=Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi_{i}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)
.
Otherwise, we have
Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)>0
and get:

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}}}\!\left(\pi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle~=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}}}\!\left(\pi_{i}^{\prime}\right)\cdot{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}(\pi_{i}^{\prime},\alpha_{i})\cdot\mathbf{P}_{i}(\mathit{last}(\pi_{i}^{\prime}),\alpha_{i},s_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)\cdot{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}}}(\pi_{i}^{\prime},\alpha_{i})\cdot\mathbf{P}_{i}(\mathit{last}(\pi_{i}^{\prime}),\alpha_{i},s_{i})

(By induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\hat{s}_{i}\in S_{i}}Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left((\pi^{\prime}_{i},\alpha_{i},\hat{s}_{i})\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}_{i}(\mathit{last}(\pi_{i}^{\prime}),\alpha_{i},s_{i})

(By
Section

3.1
, simplify)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in(\pi^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i})}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\hat{\alpha}_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2})\in\mathit{Act}_{\parallel},\\
\hat{\alpha}_{i}=\alpha_{i}\end{subarray}}Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\cdot\sigma(\pi^{\prime},(\hat{\alpha}_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2}))\cdot\mathbf{P}_{i}(\mathit{last}(\pi_{i}^{\prime}),\alpha_{i},s_{i})

(By
3
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left((\pi^{\prime}_{i},\alpha_{i},s_{i})\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\otimes\mathcal{N}_{3-i}\right).

Proof A.3
.

We show
{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}={{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}
.
The proof for
{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!2}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}={{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!2}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}^{\prime}}}}}
is symmetric.
For simplicity, we write
\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]=\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}_{1}]\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}_{2}]
.
Let
\pi_{1}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}^{\mathit{fin}}
and
\alpha_{1}\in\mathit{Act}_{1}
.
Our restrictions for
\mathpzc{v}_{1}
and
\mathpzc{v}_{1}^{\prime}
yield
Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\right)=0
iff
Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\right)=0
.
Consequently,
Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\right)=0
implies
{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1})=0={{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1}^{\prime},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1})
.
Otherwise, for
\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}\in\{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}\}
we have by
Section

3.1
:

{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1})=\frac{\sum_{s_{1}\in S_{1}}Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left((\pi_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\right)}{Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\right)}.

We show that all transition probabilities
\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}]
cancel out in the above fraction, i.e.,
{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1})
is independent of the valuation
\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}\in\{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}\}
, which concludes the proof.

We first introduce some auxiliary notations.
For
\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathit{fin}}
, let
\mathit{pref}(\pi,m)\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathit{fin}}
be the prefix of
\pi
of length
m\leq|\pi|
. The set of minimal paths
\pi
with projection
{\pi{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}}=\pi_{1}
is

\min\{\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\}=\big\{\pi\in(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2})\mathbin{\big|}\mathit{pref}(\pi,m)\notin(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2})\text{ for all }m<|\pi|\big\}.

Any path
\pi=(s_{1}^{0},s_{2}^{0}),(\alpha_{1}^{0},\alpha_{2}^{0}),(s_{1}^{1},s_{2}^{1}),\dots,(s_{1}^{n},s_{2}^{n})\in(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2})
has a prefix
\mathit{pref}(\pi,m)\in\min\{\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\}
and the subsequent transitions all correspond to asynchronous steps of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, i.e.,
s_{1}^{m}=s_{1}^{m+1}=\dots=s_{1}^{n}
and
\alpha_{1}^{m},\alpha_{1}^{m+1},\dots,\alpha_{1}^{n-1}\notin\mathit{Act}_{1}
.
Consequently, if
\pi
is initial:

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<m\\
\alpha_{1}^{j}\in\mathit{Act}_{1}\end{subarray}}\!\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1}^{j},\alpha_{1}^{j},s_{1}^{j+1})[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}]~\cdot\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<n\\
\alpha_{2}^{j}\in\mathit{Act}_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2}^{j},\alpha_{2}^{j},s_{2}^{j+1})[\mathpzc{v}_{2}]~\cdot\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<n\end{subarray}}\!\sigma(\mathit{pref}(\pi,j),(\alpha_{1}^{j},\alpha_{2}^{j}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}]~\cdot\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<n\\
\alpha_{2}^{j}\in\mathit{Act}_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2}^{j},\alpha_{2}^{j},s_{2}^{j+1})[\mathpzc{v}_{2}]~\cdot\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<n\end{subarray}}\!\sigma(\mathit{pref}(\pi,j),(\alpha_{1}^{j},\alpha_{2}^{j})),

where we set

\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}]=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<k\end{subarray}}\!\mathbf{P}_{1}(\hat{s}_{1}^{j},\hat{\alpha}_{1}^{j},\hat{s}_{1}^{j+1})[\hat{\mathpzc}{v}_{1}]
for
\pi_{1}=\hat{s}_{1}^{0},\hat{\alpha}_{1}^{0},\hat{s}_{1}^{1},\dots,\hat{s}_{1}^{k}
.
This yields

\frac{Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\mathpzc{v}_{1}]}=\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<n\\
\alpha_{2}^{j}\in\mathit{Act}_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2}^{j},\alpha_{2}^{j},s_{2}^{j+1})[\mathpzc{v}_{2}]~\cdot\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j<n\end{subarray}}\!\sigma(\mathit{pref}(\pi,j),(\alpha_{1}^{j},\alpha_{2}^{j}))=\frac{Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}_{1}]}.

Finally, we combine the above observation with
3
to conclude

\displaystyle{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\mathpzc{v}_{1}]}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\mathpzc{v}_{1}]}\cdot\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2})}~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathit{Act}_{\parallel}\end{subarray}}Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)\cdot\sigma(\pi,(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))}{\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\min\{\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\}}Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in(\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2})}~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathit{Act}_{\parallel}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\mathpzc{v}_{1}]}\cdot\sigma(\pi,(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))}{\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\min\{\pi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}\}}\dfrac{Pr_{\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}]}^{\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\pi_{1})[\mathpzc{v}_{1}]}}={{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}^{\prime}_{1},\mathpzc{v}_{2}}}}}(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1}).

We show that the verification of safety objectives reduces to maximal reachability properties in a pPA-DFA Product based on
[
44
, Lemma 1]
.
First, we lift the DFA product (see
[
44
, Definition 12]
) to pPA.

{defi}

Given a bad prefix automaton
\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}
with
\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
,
the pPA
\mathcal{M}_{{\otimes}}=(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}})
is defined as
\mathcal{M}_{{\otimes}}=\left(S_{{\otimes}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{{\otimes}}},V_{{\otimes}},\mathit{Act}_{{\otimes}},\mathbf{P}_{{\otimes}},L_{{\otimes}}\right)
,
where

•

S_{{\otimes}}=S_{\mathcal{M}}\times S_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}
,

•

{s^{\mathit{init}}_{{\otimes}}}=({s^{\mathit{init}}_{\mathcal{M}}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}}})
,

•

V_{{\otimes}}=V_{\mathcal{M}}
,

•

\mathit{Act}_{{\otimes}}=\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}}
,

•

for each
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}})
, with
L_{\mathcal{M}}(s,\alpha)=\mathsf{a}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}
and
p\in S_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}}
with
q=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}}(p,\mathsf{a})
:

\mathbf{P}_{{\otimes}}((s,q),\alpha)=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(s,\alpha)\times\mathbf{1}\quad\text{and}\quad L_{{\otimes}}((s,q),\alpha)=\mathsf{a}

for each
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}})
, with
L_{\mathcal{M}}(s,\alpha)=\mathsf{a}\not\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}
and
p\in S_{\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}}
:

\mathbf{P}_{{\otimes}}((s,q),\alpha)=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(s,\alpha)\times\mathbf{1}_{p}\quad\text{and}\quad L_{{\otimes}}((s,q),\alpha)=\mathsf{a}

It holds that
(\mathcal{M}{\otimes}\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}})[\mathpzc{v}]={\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}]\otimes\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}}
.
Using this, we can lift
[
44
, Lemma 1]
to pPAs.

Lemma 33
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a pPA and let
\mathcal{L}
be the language of a safety objective for
{\mathcal{M}}
and let
\mathpzc{v}
be a well-defined valuation.
Let
\star\in\{\mathit{prt},\mathit{cmp}\}
.

•

There is a bijection
f
between the strategies

Str_{\!\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\star}
of
\mathcal{M}
and the strategies
Str_{\!{\otimes}[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\star}
of pPA-DFA product
{\otimes}=\mathcal{M}{\otimes}\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}

•

Let
bad_{\mathcal{L}}=\{(s,q)\in S_{{\otimes}}\mid q\in F\}
= set of product-states that contain a final state of
\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\!\star}
:

Pr_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=1-Pr_{{\otimes}}^{\mathpzc{v},f(\sigma)}\!\left(\Diamond bad_{\mathcal{L}}\right)

Remark 34
.

For actions that do not appear in
\mathcal{L}
(and thus not appear in
\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}
) the state of the automaton remains unchanged.
This means paths
\pi

\mathcal{M}
such that
{\pi{\upharpoonright}_{\!\mathcal{L}}}
is finit cannot reach the
bad_{\mathcal{L}}
-labeled state in
(\mathcal{M}{\otimes}\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}})
.
Thus, for correctness of
33
, safety objectives need to be prefix-closed.

Since

\displaystyle\subseteq\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{(\mathit{safe})}}\cup\Sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\subseteq\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}}.
