Proof A.8
.

The proof is based on the proof of

We show the proof rule on the right.
For the rule on the left the proof works analogously.

Since
\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\cup\Sigma_{1}

.

{{{R}_{1}\!\cap{}{R}_{2}\!\cap{}{R}_{3}}}=\emptyset

holds,
which directly implies

•

follows

\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{3}\ }}\!\!}\textsf{A}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
.
Together with
\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}\in 2^{\Sigma_{2}}
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}]\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{3}\ }}\!\!}\textsf{A}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]

(4)

Let
\sigma\in Str_{\!(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\!{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}\ }}\!\!}
.
From
5

{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!2}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}

{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{3}\ }}\!\!
strategy of
\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}]
.
Thus, by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}]

\displaystyle\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{3}\ }}\!\!}\textsf{A}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!2}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\models{}\!{\textsf{A}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]}

We continue the proof from the main paper, which already established the left rule for partial strategies. For fair strategies the proof is similar. However, to deduce that

Proofs of

8.2

We first introduce an auxiliary result
[
68
, Lemma 4.1.2]
that is needed for
31
:

Proposition 35
.

Let
\mu_{i}\in\mathit{Dist}(S_{i}){}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and let

\varrho{}_{1}\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
,

\varrho{}_{2}\subseteq S_{2}\times S_{3}
be relations such that

\mu_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\varrho_{1}}\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{2}\sqsubseteq_{\varrho_{2}}\mu_{3}
.
Then
\mu_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\varrho}\mu_{3}
, for
\varrho=\{(s_{1},s_{3})\mid\exists s_{2}\in S_{2}:(s_{1},s_{2})\in\varrho{}_{1}\wedge(s_{2},s_{3})\in\varrho{}_{2}\}.

See
31

Proof A.10
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2},\mathcal{M}_{3}
be pPAs and let
{R}
be a region that is well-defined for all three pPAs.
We first show the properties for
\preceq_{{R}}
and then for
\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}
.

Case
\trianglelefteq=\preceq
.

•

Preorder.

For reflexivity, fix
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
.
By
27
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
for all
\mathpzc{v}
, hence
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

For transitivity, assume
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{3}
.
By definition, for each
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
we have
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{3}[\mathpzc{v}]
, and transitivity of strong simulation on PAs (
27
) yields
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{3}[\mathpzc{v}]
for all
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
.
Thus
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{3}
.

•

Compositionality.

Assume
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a pPA for which
{R}
is well-defined.
For every
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]
by assumption, and by compositionality of strong simulation on PAs (
27
),

\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\parallel\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}]\preceq\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]\parallel\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}].

Hence,
\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}\preceq_{{R}}\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M}
.

Case
\trianglelefteq=\preceq
.

•

Preorder.

Reflexivity follows by taking the identity relation
\varrho_{id}=\{(s,s)\mid s\in S_{1}\}
. This is a witness relation for
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

For transitivity, assume
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}
witnessed by the robust-strong simulation
\varrho_{1}\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{3}
witnessed by
\varrho_{2}\subseteq S_{2}\times S_{3}
.
Define
\varrho\subseteq S_{2}\times S_{3}
as follows:

\varrho=\{(s_{1},s_{3})\mid\exists s_{2}\in S_{2}:(s_{1},s_{2})\in\varrho_{1}\text{ and }(s_{2},s_{3})\in\varrho_{2}\}.

We show that
\varrho
is a strong simulation for
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{3}[\mathpzc{v}]
for all
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
by checking the clauses of
Section

8.1
.
Fix an arbitrary
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
.

–

Since
({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}})\in\varrho_{1}
and
({s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{3}})\in\varrho_{2}
, we have
({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{3}})\in\varrho
by definition of
\varrho
.

–

Let
(s_{1},s_{3})\in\varrho
and let
(s_{1},\alpha_{1})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{1})
be a transition of
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
.
We show there is a transition
(s_{3},\alpha_{3})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{3})
such that

L_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})=L_{3}(s_{3},\alpha_{3})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})[\mathpzc{v}]\sqsubseteq_{\varrho}\mathbf{P}_{3}(s_{3},\alpha_{3})[\mathpzc{v}].

Since
(s_{1},s_{3})\in\varrho
, there exists
s_{2}
with
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\varrho_{1}
and
(s_{2},s_{3})\in\varrho_{2}
.
Because
\varrho_{1}
is a robust-strong simulation, for the transition
(s_{1},\alpha_{1})
of
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
there exists a matching transition
(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{2})
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]
such that

L_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})=L_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})[\mathpzc{v}]\sqsubseteq_{\varrho_{1}}\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})[\mathpzc{v}].

Similarly, since
\varrho_{2}
is a robust-strong simulation and
(s_{2},s_{3})\in\varrho_{2}
, there exists a matching transition
(s_{3},\alpha_{3})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{3})
of
\mathcal{M}_{3}[\mathpzc{v}]
such that

L_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})=L_{3}(s_{3},\alpha_{3})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{P}_{2}(s_{2},\alpha_{2})[\mathpzc{v}]\sqsubseteq_{\varrho_{2}}\mathbf{P}_{3}(s_{3},\alpha_{3})[\mathpzc{v}].

Combining the equalities yields
L_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})=L_{3}(s_{3},\alpha_{3})
.
Moreover, by
35
and the reasoning above, we obtain

\mathbf{P}_{1}(s_{1},\alpha_{1})[\mathpzc{v}]\sqsubseteq_{\varrho}\mathbf{P}_{3}(s_{3},\alpha_{3})[\mathpzc{v}].

•

Compositionality.

Assume
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}
via a relation
\varrho
, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a pPA for which
{R}
is well-defined.
Define

\varrho_{\parallel}=\{((s_{1},s),(s_{2},s))\mid s\in S_{\mathcal{M}}\text{ and }(s_{1},s_{2})\in\varrho\}.

For each
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
, the relation
\varrho
is a strong simulation between
\mathcal{M}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}[\mathpzc{v}]
.
By compositionality of strong simulation on PAs (
27
),
\varrho_{\parallel}
is a strong simulation between the composed PAs
(\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M})[\mathpzc{v}]
and
(\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M})[\mathpzc{v}]
.
Since this holds for all
\mathpzc{v}\in{R}
, we obtain
\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}\preceq_{{R}}^{robust}\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M}
.

See
32

Proof A.11
.

The proof is analogous to the PA case in
[
42
, Theorem 1]
.
Completeness follows directly by instantiating the assumption component with
\mathcal{M}_{A}=\mathcal{M}_{1}
and commutativity of the parallel composition
\parallel
.

For soundness, assume the premises hold and fix
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}\in\{\preceq,\preceq^{robust}\}
.
From
\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{A}
and
\Sigma_{A}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}
, compositionality of
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}_{1}}
(
31
) yields

\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\mathcal{M}_{A}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{A}.

Together with the second premise
\mathcal{M}_{2}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{A}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{R}_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{G}
and transitivity of
\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}
(see
31
), we obtain

\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}\mathbin{\trianglelefteq}_{{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\mathcal{M}_{G}.

A.6.
Proofs of
Section

7

We provide the technical details underlying the results of
Section

7.3
.
We establish an equivalence between convex rPAs and their PA-reductions (cf.
Section

7.3
) with respect to safety multi-objective queries and AG triples.
Then, we use these equivalence results to prove the assume–guarantee rules for convex rPAs under the convexity-preserving composition operator from
Section

7.3
.

We define the PA-reduction of an (in)finite path
\pi=s_{0},\alpha_{0},s_{1},\alpha_{1},\dots
of
\mathcal{U}
, with states
s_{i}\in S
and actions
\alpha_{i}\in\mathit{Act}
, as the (possibly uncountable) set of PA-paths

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)=\{s_{0},(\alpha_{0},\mu_{0}),s_{1},(\alpha_{1},\mu_{1}),\ldots\mid\forall i\text{ with }0\leq i<|\pi|:\mu_{i}\in\mathit{gen}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s_{i},\alpha_{i})\right)\}.

We lift this notation to sets
X
of (in)finite paths by setting

\mathit{PA}\!\left(X\right)=\bigcup_{\pi\in X}\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right).

The models
{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
and
{\mathcal{U}}
are equivalent in the sense that they satisfy exactly the same safety multi-objective queries and AG triples.
This equivalence is obtained by the following lemmas:

•

37
shows that the behaviour of a convex rPA over-approximates the behaviour of its PA-reduction, in the sense that for every strategy on
{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
there exists a nature–strategy pair on
{\mathcal{U}}
inducing the same probability measure.

•

39
shows the converse, namely that the behaviour of the PA-reduction over-approximates that of the rPA, by constructing for every nature–strategy pair on
{\mathcal{U}}
a corresponding strategy on
{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
inducing the same probability measure.

Taken together, these results establish the equivalence between
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
, which is needed for reducing AG proof rules for rPAs to the AG framework for PAs by Kwiatkowska et al.
[
44
]
.
Similar results for polytopic rMDPs and single-objective optimisation for a stochastic game reduction appear, e.g., in
[
50
,
33
,
66
]
.
Here we obtain an analogous result for convex (not necessarily polytopic) rPAs in a cooperative setting and for safety multi-objective queries.

We formalize the relationship between the sets of paths of
\mathcal{U}
and its PA-reduction whose traces lie in a given language
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an rPA and let
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{\infty}
be a language over
\Sigma
. Then,

\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}=\mathit{PA}\!\left({\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}}\right).

Proof A.12
.

We prove both inclusions separately.

•

For the “
\subseteq
”-direction, let
\pi^{\prime}\in\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}.

By definition of the transition function
\mathbf{P}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
of the PA-reduction
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
, there exists a path
\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime\prime}\right)
.
Moreover, by the definition of the labeling function
L_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
, the trace over
\Sigma
is preserved, i.e.,

{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}={\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}.

Since
{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}
holds by assumption, it follows that
{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}
.
Hence
\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}
with
{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}
, and
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime\prime}\right)
.
This implies

\pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right).

•

For the “
\supseteq
”-direction, let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right).

By definition of the PA-reduction on sets of paths, we have

\pi^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{{\mathcal{U}}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\\
{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}}\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right).

This means there is

\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}
such that
{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime\prime}\right)
.
Again, by the definition of the labeling
L_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
of the PA-reduction, we have

{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}={\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}.

Since
{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}
, we obtain that also
{\mathit{tr}({\pi^{\prime}}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}
.
Thus,
\pi^{\prime}\in\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}
.

Since both inclusions hold, the two sets are equal, which concludes the proof.

The following lemma directly implies that, if
{\mathcal{U}}
satisfies a (multi-objective) property or AG triple, then
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
does so as well.

Lemma 37
.

Let
\star\in\{\mathit{prt},\mathit{cmp}\}
and let
\mathcal{U}
be a convex rPA.
For every strategy
\widehat{\sigma}\in Str_{\!\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\!\star}
there exist a nature
\nu\in\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}
and a strategy
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}
such that, for every language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq{\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}}
:

Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right).

Proof A.13
.

Let
\widehat{\sigma}\in Str_{\!\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\!\star}
.
We construct a strategy
\sigma
and a nature
\nu
for
\mathcal{U}
such that the induced probability measures coincide on all languages over
\Sigma
.

Remark 38
.

The sets
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)
may be uncountable.
However, by construction, all transition distributions
\mathbf{P}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}(s,(\alpha,\mu))=\mu
and all strategy choices
\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi})
are discrete distributions and thus have at most countable support.
The same will hold for
\sigma(\pi)
and
\nu(\pi,\alpha)
defined below.
Hence the induced (sub-)probability measures on infinite paths of
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
and
\mathcal{U}
are well defined via the standard cylinder-set construction.

We define
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}
such that, for
\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
:

\displaystyle\sigma(\pi,\alpha)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{s\in S}Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi,\alpha,s\right)\right)}{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)}&\text{if }{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)}>0\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Intuitively,
\sigma(\pi,\alpha)
is the conditional probability of choosing action
\alpha
after
\pi
under the PA-strategy
\widehat{\sigma}
, given that
\pi
has positive probability under
\widehat{\sigma}
.
If
\widehat{\sigma}
is a complete strategy, then
\sigma
is complete as well.

We define
\nu\in\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}
by setting, for
\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
,
\nu(\pi,\alpha)=\mu
, where

\displaystyle\mu=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi,\alpha,s\right)\right)}{\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi,\alpha,s^{\prime}\right)\right)}&\text{if }{\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi,\alpha,s^{\prime}\right)\right)}>0\\
\text{arbitrary }\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbf{P}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Intuitively, in the first case
\nu(\pi,\alpha)
chooses a distribution equal to the normalised weighted sum of distributions consistent with the choice of
\widehat{\sigma}
after
(\pi,\alpha)
.
Such a
\mu
always exists since each uncertainty set
\mathbf{P}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)
is convex and non-empty. In the second case the value of
\nu(\pi,\alpha)
is irrelevant, as the corresponding prefix has zero probability under
\widehat{\sigma}
.

We show that for every
\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
,

\displaystyle Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right).

(5)

The proof is by induction on the length of
\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}
.
For
|\pi|=0
, i.e.,
\pi={s^{\mathit{init}}}
, we have
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)=\{{s^{\mathit{init}}}\}
and hence,

Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\{{s^{\mathit{init}}}\}\right)=1=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left({s^{\mathit{init}}}\right).

For the induction step, assume
Equation

5
holds for
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
and consider
\pi=\pi^{\prime},\alpha,s\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
.
If
Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)=0
, then the induction hypothesis implies
Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\right)=0
, and since no extension of a zero-probability prefix has positive probability, we have

Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left({\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)=0
.
Otherwise,
Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)>0
and thus also
Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\right)>0
by the induction hypothesis.
We then obtain:

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)

\displaystyle~=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\cdot\sigma(\pi^{\prime},\alpha)\cdot\nu(\pi^{\prime},\alpha)(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\right)\cdot\sigma(\pi^{\prime},\alpha)\cdot\nu(\pi^{\prime},\alpha)(s)

(By induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\right)\cdot\frac{\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha,s^{\prime}\right)\right)}{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)\right)}\cdot\frac{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha,s\right)\right)}{\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha,s^{\prime}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha\right)\right)\cdot\frac{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha,s\right)\right)}{Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi^{\prime},\alpha,s\right)\right)

This proves
Equation

5
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language over
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
.
Using
36
and
Equation

5
, we obtain

\displaystyle Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right)\right)

(by
36
)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right)

(by
Equation

5
)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)

The following result implies that, if
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
satisfies a (multi-objective) property or AG triple, then
\mathcal{U}
does so as well.

Lemma 39
.

Let
\star\in\{\mathit{prt},\mathit{cmp}\}
and let
\mathcal{U}
be a convex rPA.
For every nature
\nu\in\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}
and every strategy
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}
there exists
a strategy
\widehat{\sigma}\in Str_{\!\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\!\star}
such that, for every language
\mathcal{L}
over
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
,

Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right).

Proof A.14
.

We show the claim for the case that
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)=\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)\right)
, for all

s\in S
, and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
.
When,
\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\not=\mathit{conv}\!\left(\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha)\right)\right)
, for some
s\in S
, and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}
the proof works similarly—without without decompising the distributions chosen by nature into extreme points.

Let
\nu\in\mathit{Nat}_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!}
and
{\sigma}\in Str_{\!\mathcal{U}}^{\!\star}
.
We construct a strategy
\widehat{\sigma}\in Str_{\!\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\!\star}
for
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
:
For each finite path
\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
and each action
(\alpha,\mu)\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}
, let
\pi
be the (unique) finite path of
\mathcal{U}
such that
\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)
.

Since
\mathcal{U}
is convex and
S
is finite, for every
\nu(\pi,\alpha)\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)
, there is a finite convex decomposition into extreme points of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)
: There exist a finite set
E^{\pi,\alpha}\subseteq\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)\right)
and weights
w^{\pi,\alpha}_{\mu^{\prime}}\in[0,1]
for
\mu^{\prime}\in E^{\pi,\alpha}
such that

\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\in E^{\pi,\alpha}}w^{\pi,\alpha}_{\mu^{\prime}}=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu(\pi,\alpha)=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}\in E^{\pi,\alpha}}w^{\pi,\alpha}_{\mu^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{\prime}.

We extend these weights to all
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathit{ext}\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathit{last}(\pi),\alpha)\right)
by setting
w^{\pi,\alpha}_{\mu^{\prime}}
to
0
if
\mu^{\prime}\notin E^{\pi,\alpha}
.

Then, we define
\widehat{\sigma}
such that

\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi},(\alpha,\mu))=\sigma(\pi,\alpha)\cdot w^{\pi,\alpha}_{\mu}.

Then,
\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi})
is always a discrete (countably supported) (sub-)distribution over enabled transitions.
Additionally, if
\sigma
is a complete strategy, then so is
\widehat{\sigma}
.

We show that, for every finite path
\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
:

\displaystyle Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right).

(6)

Let
\pi=s_{0},\alpha_{0},\dots,s_{n}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
.
The set
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)
can be uncountable, since each
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{U}}(s_{i},\alpha_{i})
may be uncountable.
However, all
\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)
are finite, and for each finite path
\widehat{\pi}^{\prime}
the strategy
\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi}^{\prime})
is a discrete (sub)distribution.
Therefore, only countably many
\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)
have positive probability under
\widehat{\sigma}
.
We denote this countable subset by

\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)=\{\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\mid Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\widehat{\pi}\right)>0\}.

Then,
Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)

and hence,

\displaystyle Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)\\
\widehat{\pi}=s_{0},(\alpha_{0},\mu_{0}),\dots,s_{n}\end{subarray}}Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\widehat{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)\\
\widehat{\pi}=s_{0},(\alpha_{0},\mu_{0}),\dots,s_{n}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\,s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}\,\rrbracket\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi}[0,i],(\alpha_{i},\mu_{i}))\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}(s_{i},(\alpha_{i},\mu_{i}),s_{i+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)\\
\widehat{\pi}=s_{0},(\alpha_{0},\mu_{0}),\dots,s_{n}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\,s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}\,\rrbracket\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi}[0,i],(\alpha_{i},\mu_{i}))\cdot\mu_{i}(s_{i+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\pi}\in\mathit{PA}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\pi\right)\\
\widehat{\pi}=s_{0},(\alpha_{0},\mu_{0}),\dots,s_{n}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\,s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}\,\rrbracket\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\sigma(\pi[0,i],\alpha_{i})\cdot w^{{\pi[0,i]},\alpha_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}\cdot\mu_{i}(s_{i+1})

(By definition of
\widehat{\sigma}
)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\,s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}\,\rrbracket\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(\sigma(\pi[0,i],\alpha_{i})\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E^{\pi[0,i],\alpha_{i}}}w^{\pi[0,i],\alpha_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}\cdot\mu_{i}(s_{i+1})\right)

(By definition of
\nu
)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\,s_{0}={s^{\mathit{init}}}\,\rrbracket\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\sigma(\pi[0,i],\alpha_{i})\cdot\nu(\pi[0,i],\alpha_{i})(s_{i+1})

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\pi\right)

This proves
Equation

6
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language over
\Sigma
.
Using
36
and (
6
), we obtain

\displaystyle Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right)

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}^{\widehat{\sigma}}\!\left(\mathit{PA}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right)\right)

by
36

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma}}\in\mathcal{L}\}\right)

by
Equation

6

\displaystyle=Pr_{\mathcal{U}}^{\nu,\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)

See
23

Proof A.15
.

We prove the claim for the case that every uncertainty set is the convex hull of its extreme points. The other case works analogously without decomposing distributions into convex combinations of extreme points.

We show the claim by providing a
combined bisimulation

[
63
, Definition 5.7]
between

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
and

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
.
A combined bisimulation allows matching a single transition by a finite
convex combination of transitions with the same label; hence bisimilar PAs satisfy the same (multi-objective) queries.

By construction of the PA-reduction and of parallel composition, both PAs

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
and

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
have the same set of states

S_{1}\times S_{2}
, the same initial state

({s^{\mathit{init}}_{1}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{2}})
, where
S_{i}
is the state set of

\mathcal{U}_{i}
and the same set of labels.
Consider the relation

\varrho=\{(s,s)\mid s\in S_{1}\times S_{2}\}.

We show that
\varrho
is a combined bisimulation.

Fix a state
s=(s_{1},s_{2})
and a label
\mathsf{a}
of
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
and
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right).

In
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
, any
successor distribution from
s
with label
\mathsf{a}
is a product
distributions
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
where
\mu_{i}
is an extreme
point of the corresponding uncertainty set of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
.
In
\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}
, the uncertainty set at
s
and label

\mathsf{a}
is defined as the convex hull of precisely these product
distributions, i.e., the PA-reduction
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
then chooses a generator of this convex hull as its set of successor distributions at
s
with label
\mathsf{a}
.

Thus every product distribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
that appears as an

\mathsf{a}
-transition in
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
lies
in the convex hull of the successor distributions of

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
and can therefore be written as
a finite convex combination of those.

Conversely, every transition from
s

with label
\mathsf{a}
of
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
is distribution that is in the convex hull of the product distributions and hence is a finite convex combination of transitions in
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
.

This is exactly the condition of combined bisimulation.
The argument for non-synchronising labels is analogous.

We now show AG proof rules for the compositional verification of rPAs from
Section

7.3
.
In the remainder of this section, we fix convex rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{i}
with alphabets
\Sigma_{i}
.
See
24

Proof A.16
.

The proof is based on the reduction of convex rPAs to (possibly uncountably branching) PAs, described in
Section

7.3
.

We first show the asymmetric proof rule on the left. Assume that the premises hold, i.e.,

\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}.

By
37
, the corresponding PA-reductions satisfy

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}.

For the latter, we exploit that both orders of applying PA-reduction reduction and alphabet extension extension yield PAs with the same self-loops and therefore equivalent satisfaction of safety mo-queries and AG triples, i.e., the alphabet extension of the PA-reduction
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}
and the PA-reduction of the extension

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}\right)

satisfy the same safety mo-queries and AG triples.
The premises above are exactly those of the asymmetric AG rule for PAs
from
[
44
, Theorem 1]
, so we obtain

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

By
23
follows that
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\star}\textsf{G}
.
Finally, by
39
, this result lifts back to rPAs, and we obtain

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

We show the circular proof rule on the right. The proof proceeds analogously to that of
24
.
Assume that the premises of the circular rule hold for the convex rPAs
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, i.e.,

{\mathcal{U}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}_{2}},\quad{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}_{1}.

By
37
, the corresponding premises hold for the PA-reductions:

{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}_{2}},\quad{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}_{1}.

Here we again use that the alphabet extension of the PA-reductions and the PA-reductions of the extension satisfy the same safety mo-queries and AG triples.
These are exactly the premises of the circular AG rule for PAs from

[
44
, Theorem 5]
, which yields

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

By
23
follows that
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\models^{\star}\textsf{G}
.
Finally, by
39
, this result lifts back to rPAs, and we obtain

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

Appendix B
Partial vs. Complete Strategies

Section

B.1
section establishes that verifying probabilistic, and reward objectives under partial strategies in a given pPA is equivalent to verifying them under complete strategies in an extended pPA.
This result is extended to multiobjective queries in
Section

B.2
.

B.1.
Equivalence w.r.t. Probabilistic and Reward Objectives

Next, we define the pPA
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
which extends the pPA
\mathcal{M}
by introducing transitions from each state to a new sink state which are labeled by a fresh alphabet symbol
\tau
.
We show that the class of partial strategies of a pPA
\mathcal{M}
is equivalent to the complete strategies of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.

{defi}

Given a pPA
\mathcal{M}=\left(S_{\mathcal{M}},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{\mathcal{M}}},V_{\mathcal{M}},\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}},L_{\mathcal{M}}\right)
, we define the pPA
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}=(S_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{s_{\tau}\},{s^{\mathit{init}}_{\mathcal{M}}},V_{\mathcal{M}},\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{\tau\},\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}},L_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}})
, where

•

for each
(s,\alpha)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}})
:

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(s,\alpha)=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(s,\alpha)\text{ and }L_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(s,\alpha)=L_{\mathcal{M}}(s,\alpha),

•

for each
s\in S_{\mathcal{M}}
:

\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(s,\tau)=\mathbf{1}_{s_{\tau}}\text{ and }L_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(s,\tau)=\tau.

By definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
follows, that a valuation is well-defined (graph-preserving) for pPA
\mathcal{M}
iff it is well-defined (graph-preserving) for
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.
From the following theorem follows that verifying objectives w.r.t partial strategies in
\mathcal{M}
is equivalent to verifying objectives w.r.t complete strategies in
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
,
i.e.,
\mathcal{M},{R}\models^{(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}\varphi\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\tau},{R}\models^{(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}
.

40
is based on the proof of
[
44
, Proposition 2]
.
However, we modified the construction such that the memoryless property is preserved.

Lemma 40
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language and
\mathcal{R}
be a (parametric) reward function over
\Sigma
with
\tau\not\in\Sigma
.

•

For any strategy
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}
there is a strategy
\sigma_{\tau}\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}

such that for any valuation
\mathpzc{v}
that is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(and
\mathcal{R}
)

Pr_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad Ex_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right)=Ex_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right).

•

For any strategy
\sigma_{\tau}\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}
there is a strategy
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}

such that for any valuation
\mathpzc{v}
that is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(and
\mathcal{R}
)

Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad Ex_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right)=Ex_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right).

Intuitively, all “unused” probabilities of a partial strategy of
\mathcal{M}
are redirected to a sink state by the complete strategy of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.
In
[
44
, proposition 2]
,
\tau
-labeled self-loops are introduced.
However, the resulting complete strategy requires memory.

Proof B.1
.

First, given a strategy
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}
, we construct a strategy
\sigma_{\tau}\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}
as follows:
We can apply the reasoning provided in the proof of
[
44
, proposition 2]
to our construction:
Given a finite path
\pi
of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\tau}(\pi,\alpha)=\begin{cases}\sigma(\pi,\alpha)&\text{if $\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}$ and $\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}}$}\\
1-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}}}\sigma(\pi,\alpha)&\text{if $\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}$ and }\alpha=\tau\\
1&\text{if $\pi\not\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}$ and }\alpha=\tau\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Now, for any valuation
\mathpzc{v}
that is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(and
\mathcal{R}
), we have
Pr_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)
and
Ex_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right)=Ex_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right).

Note that—in contrast to
[
44
, proposition 2]
—the strategy
\sigma_{\tau}
is memoryless if
\sigma
is a memoryless strategy.

Second, given a strategy
\sigma_{\tau}\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}
, we construct a strategy
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}
as follows:
For
{\pi}\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}\subseteq\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathit{fin}}{}
and
\alpha\in\mathit{Act}_{\mathcal{M}}
it holds that

\sigma({\pi},\alpha)=\sigma_{\tau}({\pi},\tau)
.
Again, for any valuation
\mathpzc{v}
that is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(and
\mathcal{R}
) we have
Pr_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)
and
Ex_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right)=Ex_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma_{\tau}}\!\left(\mathcal{R}\right)
.
Additionally, if
\sigma_{\tau}
is memoryless, then
\sigma
is also memoryless.

B.2.
Equivalence w.r.t. Multiobjective Queries

As a consequence of
40
, we directly obtain
[
44
, Proposition 2]
for pPA and parametric mo-queries:

Corollary 41
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a pPA and let
X
be a (parametric) multiobjective query.
Construct pPA
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
as in
Section

B.1
.

\Sigma_{\textsf{X}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
, where
\tau\not\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{X}}
.
Let
{R}
be graph-preserving well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(i.e., also for
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
) and well-defined for
X
.
Then,

\displaystyle\exists\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}:\exists\mathpzc{v}\in{R}:\mathcal{M}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models{}\!\textsf{X}[\mathpzc{v}]

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\!(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}:\exists\mathpzc{v}\in{R}:\mathcal{M}_{\tau}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models{}\!\textsf{X}[\mathpzc{v}]

B.3.
Equivalence w.r.t. Monotonicity

As a direct consequence of
40
, we obtain that monotonicity for partial strategies w.r.t general properties is equivalent to monotonicity for complete strategies in
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
(see
Section

B.1
).

Corollary 42
.

Let
{R}
be a region that is well-defined for pPA
\mathcal{M}
and let
\big\updownarrow\in\{\big\uparrow,\big\downarrow\}
.
For the solution functions
\textsf{sol}_{\mathcal{M}}{}
and
\textsf{sol}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}{}
for
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
with
\tau\not\in\Sigma_{\textsf{sol}_{\mathcal{M}}{}}
holds

{\textsf{sol}_{\mathcal{M}}}{\!\big\updownarrow}_{{{p},{{R}}}}^{(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{prt}}
iff

{\textsf{sol}_{{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}}}{\!\big\updownarrow}_{{{p},{{R}}}}^{(\mathit{mless},)\mathit{cmp}}
.

42
also holds for local monotonicity.

Appendix C
Further AG Proof Rules for pPAs

We extend the remaining proof rules from
[
44
]
to pPA. This includes
43
, which enables reasoning about the conjunction of multi-objective queries, as well as
44
, an asymmetric rule for more than two components that extends
13
.
Additionally, we introduce
45
for handling interleaving components and
46
, which supports modular reasoning about reward structures.

When the premises include
\mathcal{M},{R}\models^{\star}\textsf{A}
we adhere to the following assumptions:

•

A
is a (parametric) mo-query for
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
A
is defined over the alphabet
\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
, and

•

{R}
is a region that is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(and
A
).

Similarly, if an AG triple
{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle},{R}}\models^{\star}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}
occurs in the premises, we assume:

•

G
is a (parametric) mo-query for
\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}
, i.e.,
G
is defined over the alphabet
\Sigma_{\textsf{G}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}
7
7

7

\textsf{A}^{(\mathit{safe})}\not\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
is possible.
, and

•

the region
{R}
is well-defined for
\mathcal{M}
(
A
, and
G
).

Safety mo-queries are marked with a superscript “
\mathit{safe}
”.

To reason about the conjunction of multi-objective queries, we generalize
[
44
, Theorem 3]
for pPA.
When safety-related multi-objective queries are combined through conjunction, the resulting query remains a safety multi-objective query.

Theorem 43

(Conjunction)
.

Let
{R}_{\cap}={{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}
,
\textsf{A}_{\land}=\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{2}
, and
\textsf{G}_{\land}=\textsf{G}_{1}\land\textsf{G}_{2}
.

{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}\rangle},{R}_{1}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}

{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{\land}}{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{2}}{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle},{R}_{1}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{1}}

for
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in 2^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}}
.

Proof C.1
.

We show the statement for safety properties. For general properties and fair strategies, the proof is analogous.
First, we observe that
{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}
is a valid multiobjective query for
\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}
as

\Sigma_{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}}
holds.
Second, assume the premises hold.
As
\Sigma_{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}
,
it holds that for
i\in\{1,2\}
:

{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}\land\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle},{R}_{i}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}

From this it follows that

{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle},{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}}

As in the proof of
[
44
, Theorem 3]
, by the definition of pAG triples (
Section

5.1
),
we observe, that for
\mathpzc{v}\in{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}
and
\sigma\in Str_{\!\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle}}^{\!\mathit{prt}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in\{1,2\}}\left(\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}\land\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}\quad\rightarrow\quad\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}\land\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in\{1,2\}}\left(\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}\land\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\not\models\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}\quad\lor\quad\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}\land\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\not\models\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}\quad\lor\quad\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}\quad\rightarrow\quad\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}

Thus,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{M}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}\rangle},{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}}

By combining
13
and
43
, we obtain the
following analogue to
[
44
, Theorem 4]
—the asymmetric proof rule for
n>2
components—for pPA.

Theorem 44

(Asymmetric-N)
.

Let
{R}_{\cap}={{{R}_{1}\!\cap{}\dots\!\cap{}{R}_{n}}}
, and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

\mathcal{M},{R}_{\cap}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}{\mathcal{M}_{n}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{n-1}}\rangle},{R}_{n}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{n-1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}}\vdots{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{1},{R}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}

\mathcal{M},{R}_{\cap}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{C}_{n}\ }}\!\!}\textsf{G}{\mathcal{M}_{n}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{n-1}}\rangle},{R}_{n}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{n-1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\vdots{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{1},{R}_{1}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\ }}\!\!}\textsf{A}_{1}

for
\mathcal{C}_{1}\in 2^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in 2^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i-1}}}
, for
i>1
.

Proof C.2
.

Analogously to
[
44
, Theorem 4]
, the result follows by a repeated application of
13
and
43
.

Next, we extend
[
44
, Theorem 6]
—a rule for reasoning about properties with non-synchronized actions.

Theorem 45

(Interleaving)
.

Let
{R}_{\cap}={{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}
,
\textsf{A}_{\land}=\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{2}
,
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}
and
(\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}})\cap(\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}})=\emptyset
.

{{\mathcal{M}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\geq p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\mathit{safe}}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}^{\mathit{safe}})}{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{P}_{\geq p_{2}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{\mathit{safe}})}}{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}\rangle},{R}_{1}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\geq p_{1}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\mathit{safe}})}

{{\mathcal{M}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sim p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})}{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{P}_{\sim p_{2}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{2})}}{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle},{R}_{1}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\sim p_{1}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1})}

for
\sim\in\{<,\leq,>,\geq\}
and

\mathcal{C}_{i}\in 2^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}}
.

Proof C.3
.

The proof is based on the proof of
[
44
, Theorem 6]
.
First, we have that
{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sim p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\mathit{safe})}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\mathit{safe})})}
is a valid query for
\mathcal{M}
, since
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sim p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\mathit{safe})}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\mathit{safe})})}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
.
We show the proof of correctness of the rule involving general predicates and fair strategies.
For safety properties the proof works analogously.

If
{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}=\emptyset
, the conclusion trivially holds.
We assume
{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}\not=\emptyset
.
We show that
{{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sim p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})}

holds, which implies that

{{\mathcal{M}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sim p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})}
holds.

Let
\mathpzc{v}
be a valuation in
{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}
and
\sigma
be a
{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!
strategy of

(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})[\mathpzc{v}]
.
We show
{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]\text{ implies }{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sim p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})
.

Assume
{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{1}[\mathpzc{v}]
holds.

We have
{\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\textsf{A}_{i}\rangle},{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{i}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\sim p_{i}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{i})}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
by the premises.
Thus,

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}_{i}[\mathpzc{v}]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}],{{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\models\textsf{A}_{i}[\mathpzc{v}]

By
11

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle}[{\mathpzc{v}}],{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}\models\mathbb{P}_{\sim p_{i}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{i})

{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}\in Str_{\!{\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle}}[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\!{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}\ }}\!\!}

and
{\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle},{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{i}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\sim p_{i}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{i})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle}}^{{\mathpzc{v}},{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}_{i}\right)\sim p_{i}

(7)

We have
(\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}})\cap(\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}})=\emptyset
.
Thus,

\displaystyle Pr_{{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\mathit{inf}}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}}}}\not\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}\}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{i\in\{1,2\}}Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle}}^{\mathpzc{v},{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!i}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{M}_{i}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}\rangle}[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\mathit{inf}}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}}}\not\in\mathcal{L}_{i}\}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\left(1-Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}^{{\mathpzc{v}},{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}_{1}\right)\right)\cdot\left(1-Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle 2\rangle}}^{{\mathpzc{v}},{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!2}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}_{2}\right)\right)

Then, by
Equation

7
:

\displaystyle Pr_{{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=1-Pr_{{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}}^{\mathpzc{v},\sigma}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})[\mathpzc{v}]}^{\mathit{inf}}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}}}}\not\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}\}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\left(1-\underbrace{Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}^{{\mathpzc{v}},{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!1}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}_{1}\right)}_{\sim p_{1}}\right)\cdot\left(1-\underbrace{Pr_{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}^{{\mathpzc{v}},{{{\sigma{\upharpoonright}_{\!2}^{\mathpzc{v}}}}}}\!\left(\mathcal{L}_{2}\right)}_{\sim p_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\sim p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{1}\cdot p_{2}

Lastly, we lift
[
44
, Theorem 7]
to pPA, which reasons about the sum of two (parametric) reward functions
\mathcal{R}_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}
for
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, respectively, in the composition
\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}
.
The sum of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is reward function
(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})
on
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
over the parameter set
V_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\cup V_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
.

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})(a)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}_{1}(a)+\mathcal{R}_{2}(a),&\text{for }a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\cap\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\\
\mathcal{R}_{1}(a),&\text{for }a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\setminus\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\\
\mathcal{R}_{2}(a),&\text{for }a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\setminus\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}.\end{cases}

Theorem 46

(Reward Sum)
.

Let
{R}_{\cap}={{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}
,
\textsf{A}_{\land}=\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}
.

{{\mathcal{M}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}+r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})}{\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\textsf{A}_{2}\rangle},{R}_{2}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{2})}{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\textsf{A}_{1}\rangle},{R}_{1}}\models^{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\ }}\!\!}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1})}

for
\sim\in\{<,\leq,>,\geq\}
and

\mathcal{C}_{i}\in 2^{\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{i}}}
.

Proof C.4
.

The proof is similar to the proof of
[

, Theorem 7]
.
Since
\Sigma_{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}+r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
,
We have that
{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}+r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})}
is a valid query for
\mathcal{M}
,
If
{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}=\emptyset
, the conclusion trivially holds.
We assume
{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}\not=\emptyset
.

We show that
{{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}+r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})}}

holds, which implies that

{{\mathcal{M}},{R}_{\cap}}\models^{{{{\mathit{fair}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}\ }}\!\!}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}+r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})}}
holds.

Let

be a valuation in
{{{R}_{1}}\!\cap{}\!{{R}_{2}}}
and
\sigma

{{\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models{\mathbb{E}_{\sim r_{1}+r_{2}}\!(\mathcal{R}_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{2})}[\mathpzc{v}]

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{M}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}\parallel\mathcal{M}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle})}[\mathpzc{v}],{\sigma}\models\textsf{A}_{i}[\mathpzc{v}]

Appendix D
Further AG Proof Rules for rPAs

We lift several additional AG proof rules from the PA setting of
[
44
]
to convex rPAs under the convex composition operator
\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}
.
In particular,
47
enables reasoning about the conjunction of multi-objective queries, and
48
provides an asymmetric rule for more than two components, extending the two-component rule
24
.
In addition,
49
handles interleaving components (i.e., components without synchronising labels).
Again, we restrict to safety properties and leave general properties w.r.t. fair satisfaction to future work.

When the premises include
\mathcal{U}\models^{\star}\textsf{A}
we adhere to the following assumptions:

•

A
is a safety mo-query for
\mathcal{U}
, i.e.,
A
is defined over the alphabet
\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}

Similarly, if an AG triple
{\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}}\models^{\star}{\textsf{A}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}
occurs in the premises, we assume:

•

G
is a safety mo-query for
\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}\rangle}
, i.e.,
G
is defined over the alphabet
\Sigma_{\textsf{G}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}}
8
8

8

\textsf{A}^{(\mathit{safe})}\not\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
is possible.

To reason about the conjunction of multi-objective queries, we generalise
[
44
, Theorem 3]
for PAs (see
43
for pPAs) to rPAs.

Theorem 47

(Conjunction)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a convex rPA and let
\textsf{A}_{\land}=\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{2}
, and
\textsf{G}_{\land}=\textsf{G}_{1}\land\textsf{G}_{2}
.

{\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{\land}}{\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{2}}{\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{1}}

Proof D.1
.

Assume that the premises hold.
As in the proof of
24
, we first apply the PA-reduction for convex rPAs defined in
Section

7.3
.
And use
37
to obtain corresponding AG triples on the PA-reduction
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)
.
In particular, the premises imply that the PA-reduction satisfies the premises of the conjunction rule for PAs from
[
44
, Theorem 3]
.
Hence we obtain

{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}\right)}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{\land}}.

Then, by
39
, we deduce

{\mathcal{U}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}_{\land}}.

We now lift the asymmetric AG rule to systems with more than two components, yielding an Asym-
N
rule for convex rPAs.
This is the rPA analogue of the corresponding rule for
PAs in
[
44
, Theorem 4]
and its pPA version in
44
.

Theorem 48

(Asymmetric-N)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{U}_{n}
be convex rPAs and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\dots\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{n}
.

\mathcal{U}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}{\mathcal{U}_{n}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{n-1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{n-1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}\vdots{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}_{2}}\mathcal{U}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}_{1}

Proof D.2
.

Assume that the premises hold.
As in the proof of
24
, we first apply the PA-reduction for convex rPAs as given in
Section

7.3
.
Then, by
37
this yields corresponding premises for the asymmetric-N AG rule for PAs from
[
44
, Theorem 4]
.
More precisely,

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}_{1},\ {\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{A}_{2}},\ \dots,\text{ and }\ {\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{n}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{n-1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{n-1}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}}.

By
[
44
, Theorem 4]
, we have

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\dots\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{n}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

Then, by
23
lifted to multiple components, we obtain that

\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\dots\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{n}\right)\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

Finally, by
39
, this result lifts back to rPAs, and we obtain

\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\dots\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{n}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}.

Lastly, we consider reasoning about properties whose alphabets do not share any common labels, i.e., about interleaving behaviour.
The following rule is the rPA analogue of

[
44
, Theorem 6]
for PAs (cf.
45
for pPAs).
As above, we restrict to to safety properties: The languages
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
are safety languages, and their union
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}
is again a safety language.

Theorem 49

(Interleaving)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1},
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
be convex rPAs such that
(\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}})\cap(\Sigma_{\mathcal{U}_{2}}\cup\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}})=\emptyset
,
\textsf{A}_{\land}=\textsf{A}_{1}\land\textsf{A}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{U}=(\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathbin{\parallel^{conv}}\mathcal{U}_{2}){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{\land}}\rangle}
.

{{\mathcal{U}}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\geq p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})}{\mathcal{U}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{P}_{\geq p_{2}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{2})}}{\mathcal{U}_{1}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\geq p_{1}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1})}

Proof D.3
.

Assume that the premises hold.
Analogously to the proof of
24
, we first apply the PA-reduction for convex rPAs (
Section

7.3
) to obtain corresponding premises for the PA-reduction
\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right)\parallel\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)
with respect to the interleaving rule for PAs from
[
44
, Theorem 6]
.

{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{1}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\geq p_{1}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1})}\ \text{ and }\ {\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}_{2}\right){\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}_{2}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}{{\mathbb{P}_{\geq p_{2}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{2})}}.

By
[
44
, Theorem 6]
, and by lifting the reasoning in
23
to AG-triples, we can
deduce that

{\mathit{PA}\!\left(\mathcal{U}\right)}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\geq p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})}
.
Then, by
39
, we obtain

{{\mathcal{U}}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}_{\land}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{P}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\geq p_{1}+p_{2}\\
\ -p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\end{subarray}}\!(\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2})}

Appendix E
Differences to Kwiatkowska et al. (2013)—Explained

The safety properties in
[
44
]
are languages over infinite words, whereas we allow finite words.
This leads to the following problems in
[
44
]
:

•

[
44
, Lemma 1]
is inconsistent because
\mathcal{L}
is not prefix-closed:
In
[
44
]
, safety properties are defined over infinite words:

\mathcal{L}=\{w\in\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.8945,0.1016,0.1094}{\omega}}}\mid\text{no prefix of $w$ is in }\mathcal{L}_{err}\}
.
However, the probability of a language is defined as:

Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left({\mathcal{L}}\right)=Pr_{\mathcal{N}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8945,0.1016,0.1094}{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}\in\mathcal{L}}\}\right).

This definition does not properly account for bad prefixes.
Specifically, for a path
\pi
of a PA
\mathcal{N}
and a safety
\mathcal{L}
for
\mathcal{N}
, the fact that
{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}\not\in\mathcal{L}
does not imply that
{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}
is accepted by the bad-prefix automaton of
\mathcal{L}
.
For example, if
\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}}
, then
{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}
may be a finite prefix of a word in
\mathcal{L}
without being a bad prefix.
Since
\mathcal{L}
is not prefix-closed, this leads to situations where
\pi\not\in\{\pi\in\mathit{Paths}_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathit{inf}}{}\mid{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}\in\mathcal{L}\},
even though
{\mathit{tr}({\pi}){\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}
is not a bad prefix.

•

This inconsistency propagates to
[
44
, Proposition 1]
and thus also to the asymmetric AG-rule in
[
44
, Theorem 1]
, both of which rely on
[
44
, Lemma 1]
.

By allowing both finite and infinite words, our definition ensures that safety properties are inherently prefix-closed, resolving these issues:

\mathcal{L}=\{w\in\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.8945,0.1016,0.1094}{\infty}}}\mid\text{no prefix of $w$ is in }\mathcal{L}_{err}\}
.

s_{0}

s_{1}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}

(a)

PA
\mathcal{N}_{1}

t_{0}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}

(b)

PA
\mathcal{N}_{2}

t_{0}

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}

(c)

PA
\mathcal{N}_{2}{\langle{\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}\}}\rangle}

s_{0},t_{0}

s_{1},t_{0}

\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{b}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{c}
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{a}

(d)

PA
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}

Figure 10
.
PAs used in
Appendix

E

We present a counterexample to
[
44
, Lemma 1]
and
[
44
, Theorem 1]
, for safety properties containing only infinite words and
\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}\not=\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}}
(a similar counterexample exists if
\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}\not=\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}_{1}}
).

{exa}

Consider the safety property
\mathcal{L}=\{w\in\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}\}^{\omega}\mid|w|_{\mathsf{a}}\leq 1\}
, i.e.,
\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}={\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}}
.
Importantly,
\mathcal{L}
consists only of infinite words.
Now, consider the PAs
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
in
Figures

10(a)
and
10(b)

and their composition
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}
in
Figure

10(d)
.
We define the complete strategy
\sigma
that always chooses action
b
in
(s_{0},t_{0})
with probability 1.
Then,
Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=0
since
{b^{\omega}{\upharpoonright}_{\!\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}}=\epsilon\not\in\mathcal{L}
.
Thus, we obtain
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\not\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}
.
However, by
[
44
, Lemma 1]
, it would follow that
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}
as there is no strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
such that

1-Pr_{(\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}){\otimes}\mathcal{A}^{bad}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\!\left(\Diamond bad_{\mathcal{L}}\right)=1=Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right).

Since
Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}}^{\sigma}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)=0
, there cannot be a bijection, contradicting
[
44
, Lemma 1]
.

This also provides a counterexample to
[
44
, Theorem 1]
.
Let
\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}=\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\geq 1}\!(\mathcal{L})\}
.
For
\mathcal{N}_{1}
in
Figure

10(a)
, we have that
\mathcal{N}_{1}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}
.
Additionally, for
\mathcal{N}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}}\rangle}
in
Figure

10(c)
, we obtain
{\mathcal{N}_{2}{\langle\Sigma_{\mathcal{L}}\rangle}}\models^{\mathit{prt}}{\textsf{A}^{\mathit{safe}}}{\rightarrow}{\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}}
, which trivially holds, as it is a tautology.
By
[
44
, Theorem 1]
this implies that
\mathcal{N}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{N}_{2}\models^{\mathit{cmp}}\textsf{G}^{\mathit{safe}}
.

[
44
, Lemma 1, Theorem 1]
yields results we would expect using our definition of safety properties, which consider both finite and infinite words.
